## Where we are

**Docs and contract: complete.** `DOMAIN.md` → `DESIGN.md` → `SCOPE.md` → [types.ts](src/lib/domain/types.ts) are all written, and `DECISIONS.md` runs D-001 through D-009.

**Build: steps 1 and 2 of 8 done.** Scaffold plus one real sandbox payment (`pay_iOpVKoaAvGIt6mEyaEe5`). Tree is green — typecheck, lint, 4 tests.

What exists is genuinely thin: [client.ts](src/lib/hyperswitch/client.ts) has `createPayment` / `getPayment` / `createRefund`, [intent/route.ts](src/app/api/payments/intent/route.ts) is hardcoded to a $1.00 `SMOKE-0001`, and the landing page says so. No statements, no webhook route, no fixtures. Steps 3–8 are the whole domain.

**Environment.** Profile id and webhook secret are both in `.env.local`, so handoff items 1–2 are closed. Items 3–4 are not: `NEXT_PUBLIC_APP_URL` is still `localhost:3000` and there is no `.vercel` directory. **Not deployed, webhook endpoint not registered.** That gates step 5, which is the highest-signal step for a payments reviewer.

One piece of doc drift: `DESIGN.md` §15 still lists the auth header as an open question, but [client.ts](src/lib/hyperswitch/client.ts) resolved it to `api-key` and D-007 resolved the path empirically. Worth closing in the doc.

## The session-documentation problem

You're right to raise it, and it's worse than a to-do item.

`npm run export-sessions` today produces **one file — this conversation, started 40 minutes ago.** That is everything in `~/.claude/projects/C--Users-yun-dev-aftercare-payments/`.

The session that actually produced the domain doc, the design, the type contract, the scaffold, D-001 through D-009, and the first live payment is `b387cc9b-189f-414d-976a-3150caf80741.jsonl` — 1.87 MB, Aug 29 → Aug 31, 269 mentions of aftercare/hyperswitch. It lives under `[REDACTED]`, because that was the working directory when you ran it. The exporter keys off cwd by design ("sessions from other projects cannot leak in"), so it cannot see it.

Two consequences:

1. **The deliverable HANDOFF.md describes has no source material where the tool looks.** The index is the point, and right now there is nothing to index.
2. **That file sits in your [REDACTED] project directory.** Its first prompt is the Juspay recruiter email, which suggests the session is Juspay-scoped end to end — but I couldn't verify that, and sibling sessions in that directory are resumes, other companies' JDs, and interview prep. Exporting it into a public repo without reading it first is not something I'll do on my own judgment.

I tried to map its prompt structure to confirm it's Juspay-only; the auto-mode classifier blocked reading another project's transcript. Reasonable block. It needs your call rather than a workaround.

## Proposed order

Deploy moves up. Step 8 in the handoff is "deploy and test cold," but step 5 can't be built without a public URL, and the handoff itself says prefer deploying early over a local tunnel.

1. **Vercel + webhook registration** — unblocks everything downstream, ~20 min
2. **Step 3: fixtures and statement lookup** — pure local work against `types.ts`, the largest single chunk
3. **Step 4: real payment against a derived balance** — replaces the hardcoded `SMOKE_TEST_AMOUNT`
4. **Step 5: webhooks as source of truth** — HMAC over the raw body, idempotent on `event_id`, ordered on `updated`. The step a payments reviewer will actually read
5. **Step 6: domain flows** — health account BIN classification, split tender, partial refund
6. **Step 7: risk controls**, then **cold test on a phone**

Running alongside: append to `DECISIONS.md` as calls get made, and close the `DESIGN.md` §15 drift.

## One decision before I start

The transcript question changes what I do next, so I'd rather ask than assume.

## Done

**Exporter now reaches the real session.** Added `--project`, `--session`, and `--from` to [export-session.mjs](scripts/export-session.mjs). It warns loudly when reading outside this repo's transcript directory, because the cwd default was a safety property, not a convenience.

**Found a defect worth having.** The exporter counted every user-role message as an operator prompt. Claude Code returns tool results as user-role messages, so it reported **151 prompts for a session that had 17**, and rendered every build log as a numbered heading. Fixed. The honest number is also the better one: 17 prompts produced the domain analysis, the design, the type contract, the scaffold, and a live sandbox payment. Logged as D-010.

**Reviewed the export.** Attached above, sitting in scratchpad — nothing has entered the repo.

- **No live credentials.** Every key-shaped string came through `[REDACTED]`. The one `sk_test` hit is a discussion of the key format, not a key.
- **Unrelated material was one contiguous block**, inside prompt 1's response: compensation history, and a directory listing exposing prep files for another employer. Not scattered through the session, which is why a prompt boundary was a clean enough cut. `--from 2` removes it, and the header says prompts were withheld rather than renumbering to hide it.
- Two residual items that are your call, not mine: `yun@yunvo.fit` appears (from git config output) and `C:\Users\yun` paths appear throughout. Both are already visible on your public commits, so I'd leave them.

Logged as D-011, including why a flag beats hand-trimming: a transcript nobody can regenerate is the opposite of what sharing a session demonstrates.

Uncommitted: `docs/DECISIONS.md` (+56), `scripts/export-session.mjs` (+138/-17). Verify is green. I haven't committed — say the word.

One thing the export revealed: that session already spotted the transcript problem and told you to start the build in a session rooted at `~/dev/aftercare-payments`. That advice was followed, which is why this session is filed correctly. Good moment for the index.

## Next: deploy

This is yours to do — it needs your Vercel account.

1. vercel.com/new, import `Burgeoned/aftercare-payments`
2. Add the five server env vars from `.env.local`, plus `NEXT_PUBLIC_HYPERSWITCH_PUBLISHABLE_KEY`
3. Set `NEXT_PUBLIC_APP_URL` to the production URL, not localhost
4. Hyperswitch dashboard → Developers → Payment Settings → Webhook Setup → `https://<url>/api/webhooks/hyperswitch`

While you're in the dashboard, confirm handoff item 1: the connector is Stripe holding an `sk_test_` key, not the dummy connector. D-008 records the final config as cards plus ACH debit, but it doesn't say the connector type was verified, and dummy-connector payments can't be refunded — which would make the partial refund flow in step 6 undemonstrable.

Then I take step 3, statement fixtures and lookup against `types.ts`. That's the largest remaining chunk and entirely local, so I can start it the moment you want, deploy or no deploy.

One small thing I'll fold in: `DESIGN.md` §15 still lists the auth header as an open question, but [client.ts](src/lib/hyperswitch/client.ts) settled on `api-key` and D-007 resolved the path. Worth closing before a reviewer reads §15 and thinks it's still unknown.

**Response**

Step 3 is done and the gate passed. Screenshot above is the mixed-eligibility statement rendering.

**What's built:** [fixtures.ts](src/lib/domain/fixtures.ts) (three statements), [balance.ts](src/lib/domain/balance.ts) (derivation, pure), [lookup.ts](src/lib/domain/lookup.ts) (guest auth), [access.ts](src/lib/access.ts), [lookup/route.ts](src/app/api/statements/lookup/route.ts), [statement/[ref]/page.tsx](src/app/statement/[ref]/page.tsx). 44 tests, 5 files, verify and build both clean.

The fixture arithmetic is asserted rather than trusted — every line must satisfy `allowed = payerPaid + patientOwes`, which is the identity a real adjudication satisfies. The non-covered cosmetic line is the interesting one: allowed equals charged, because a payer that denies coverage applies no contractual adjustment, so the patient owes the full charge and no phantom discount appears.

**One thing you need to add to Vercel while you're in there:** `AFTERCARE_SESSION_SECRET`. Generate with:

```bash
node -e "console.log(require('node:crypto').randomBytes(32).toString('hex'))"
```

It's already in `.env.local` and `.env.example`. It signs the guest access cookie, and it's deliberately separate from the webhook secret — a key that authenticates a processor shouldn't also mint session tokens.

## Two things the build changed

**Lookup became a POST.** `DESIGN.md` §13 specified `GET /api/statements/:ref`. A GET carrying a date of birth writes it to browser history, the Referer header of every subsequent request, and every proxy log in between. Design doc updated, D-012 logged. I also made a missing reference and a wrong date of birth return the identical error — distinguishing them makes the endpoint an oracle for valid statement references. That has a real usability cost and I logged it as one.

**A design assumption turned out to be false, and this one matters.**

I built access grants as a `Map` in module scope. The lookup route wrote a grant; the statement page never found it. Instrumenting with a per-instance id:

```
[diag] grantAccess in 19n386 token 88c108cf
[diag] resolveAccess in fs3ykd known 0 token 88c108cf
```

Next instantiates a module **separately per layer**. A route handler and a page importing the same file get different copies of its state — same process, one machine. Vercel doesn't cause this; it makes it worse, since separate instances share nothing at all.

Fixed for access grants by making them stateless signed tokens. **Not fixed for the payment log**, and that blocks step 5: "webhooks are the source of truth" cannot hold when the source of truth is per module instance. Nothing writes to that log yet, so it's latent — it stops being latent the moment a webhook writes a status a page has to read.

Logged as D-013, including that the §12 reasoning was sound for the case it considered and wrong about the case it didn't.

## The decision this forces

This one needs you, because two of the three options add a dependency.

**Response**

Redis store is wired in behind the same boundary. Typecheck, lint, 44 tests, and `next build` all pass.

**Blocked on one thing:** I can't verify the ledger path until Upstash exists. Right now a lookup returns 500 with exactly the error it should:

```
MissingEnvError: Missing required environment variable(s):
UPSTASH_REDIS_REST_URL, UPSTASH_REDIS_REST_TOKEN
```

That's the loud failure, not a silent fallback to broken in-memory behavior.

**To unblock, in the Vercel dashboard:** Storage → Marketplace → Upstash → Redis → connect it to the project. It sets both variables on the deployment automatically. Then for local dev:

```bash
npx vercel env pull .env.local
```

That pulls them down. It will also overwrite `.env.local`, so check `AFTERCARE_SESSION_SECRET` and your Hyperswitch keys survive — add the session secret to Vercel first and they'll come back down together.

Once those land I'll re-run the flow and confirm a payment appended to Redis shows up in a page render, which is the thing that was broken.

**What changed structurally:** `paymentsForStatement` and `refundsForPayments` are now async, so `lookupStatement` and `viewStatement` are too. `deriveBalance` stayed pure and synchronous — it takes records and returns a computation, so the whole state machine is still testable with no network. The lookup tests stub the ledger; balance tests build `Payment` and `Refund` records by hand.

Refunds are keyed by payment id rather than statement id, because a refund is bound to the payment it reverses. That's what keeps health account funds returning to the account they came from.

Redis is used as an append-only log — no schema, nothing updated in place — so the `DESIGN.md` §12 argument survives intact: statement status is still derived by folding the log, never stored.

One nice piece of corroboration for D-013 fell out of a stale HMR error. Next names both module instances in its own output:

```
The export allRefunds was not found in module .../store.ts [app-route]
The export allRefunds was not found in module .../store.ts [app-rsc]
```

One file, two compiled modules, two sets of state. Added to the decision entry.

## Where things stand

Uncommitted: 12 new files, 10 modified, across the exporter work and step 3. That's a lot to be sitting in a dirty tree — want me to commit it? I'd split it into three: the exporter fixes, step 3, and the persistence change.

Still outstanding on your side: confirm the Hyperswitch connector is Stripe rather than the dummy connector, and register the webhook endpoint at `https://<vercel-url>/api/webhooks/hyperswitch` once the deploy settles.

### 3. Prompt

we can do that but first on vercel

i havent actually entered the env yet

where do i do that for the vercel deploy

**Response**

Two places, depending on where you are in the flow.

**If you're still on the import screen** (vercel.com/new, before clicking Deploy): there's an **Environment Variables** section on that configure page — expand it and add them there.

**If the project already exists:** Project → **Settings** → **Environment Variables** → add, then redeploy from the Deployments tab.

Either screen lets you paste a whole `.env` block at once rather than adding one at a time — paste into the key field and it splits them out.

## What to enter

Copy the values from your local `.env.local`:

| Variable | Value |
|---|---|
| `HYPERSWITCH_API_KEY` | from `.env.local` |
| `HYPERSWITCH_BASE_URL` | `https://sandbox.hyperswitch.io` |
| `NEXT_PUBLIC_HYPERSWITCH_PUBLISHABLE_KEY` | from `.env.local` |
| `HYPERSWITCH_PROFILE_ID` | from `.env.local` |
| `HYPERSWITCH_WEBHOOK_SECRET` | from `.env.local` |
| `AFTERCARE_SESSION_SECRET` | generate a fresh one, below |
| `NEXT_PUBLIC_APP_URL` | your Vercel production URL |

Fresh secret for production — don't reuse the local one:

```bash
node -e "console.log(require('node:crypto').randomBytes(32).toString('hex'))"
```

Tick **Production, Preview, and Development** for all of them.

Don't add the two `UPSTASH_*` variables by hand. The Upstash integration sets those itself when you connect it (Storage → Marketplace), and hand-entering them means they drift when the integration rotates them.

## Two ordering gotchas

**`NEXT_PUBLIC_APP_URL` is chicken-and-egg.** You don't know the production URL until the first deploy finishes. So: deploy once with the rest, copy the URL Vercel gives you, set `NEXT_PUBLIC_APP_URL` to it, then redeploy.

**`NEXT_PUBLIC_*` variables are inlined into the bundle at build time**, not read at runtime. Adding or changing either of those two does nothing until you redeploy. The server-side ones (`HYPERSWITCH_API_KEY`, `AFTERCARE_SESSION_SECRET`, and the rest) are read per request, so they take effect without a rebuild — but redeploy anyway so everything is consistent.

The first deploy will build fine even with nothing set — `serverEnv()` is called inside request handlers rather than at module scope, deliberately, so a missing variable surfaces as a readable 500 on the route that needs it instead of a build failure with a stack trace. So don't read a green build as confirmation the env is right.

**Response**

Diagnosis confirmed, and the error is more precise than it looks.

The deployment is reporting exactly **five** missing variables. The code in this session's working tree requires **six** — it also needs `AFTERCARE_SESSION_SECRET`. The five in your error are exactly what commit `a75210d` requires.

So the running deployment is `a75210d`, from before this session. Nothing I've built today is committed, let alone deployed. That part is expected.

**The actual problem: environment variables bind to a deployment when it's built.** Adding them afterward does not reach back into a deployment that already exists. Yours were added after that build ran, so the function has none of them — which is why all five report missing despite the dashboard showing them.

## Fix

Deployments tab → the current production deployment → **⋯** → **Redeploy**. **Uncheck "Use existing Build Cache."** The cache matters because `NEXT_PUBLIC_APP_URL` and `NEXT_PUBLIC_HYPERSWITCH_PUBLISHABLE_KEY` are inlined into the bundle at build time; a cached build can reuse the old bundle with the old (absent) values.

**Check `NEXT_PUBLIC_APP_URL` before you redeploy.** If it's still `http://localhost:3000` from a copy-paste, the 3DS return URL will send patients to their own machine. It needs to be your Vercel production URL.

## Two things while you're in there

Add **`AFTERCARE_SESSION_SECRET`** now — the moment we commit this session's work, the app requires it and you'll get this same error again for that one variable:

```bash
node -e "console.log(require('node:crypto').randomBytes(32).toString('hex'))"
```

And connect **Upstash** (Storage → Marketplace → Upstash Redis). Same reason: the committed code will need it.

Also tick **Development** on the variables, not just Production and Preview. It costs nothing now and it's what `vercel env pull` reads — with Development empty, pulling would hand you a file missing every key and wipe your working `.env.local`.

Once the redeploy is green, `/pay` should create a real sandbox payment again. Tell me when it's up and I'll commit this session's work so the statement flow actually deploys.

i see im editing them now but:

Remove the public framework prefix to keep this value private. Public prefixes expose values to the browser. If that’s safe, change the variable to Config.

this is for NEXT_PUBLIC_HYPERSWITCH_PUBLISHABLE_KEY

**Response**

> **Tool: `Bash`**
>
> ```json
> {"command":"grep -E '^(HYPERSWITCH_API_KEY|NEXT_PUBLIC_HYPERSWITCH_PUBLISHABLE_KEY)=' .env.example && echo \"--- local values, prefix only ---\" && grep -E '^(HYPERSWITCH_API_KEY|NEXT_PUBLIC_HYPERSWITCH_PUBLISHABLE_KEY)=' .env.local | sed -E 's/^([A-Z_]+)=([a-z_]*_?)[A-Za-z0-9]{4}.*/\\1 starts with: \\2xxxx/'","description":"Check publishable vs secret key prefixes"}
> ```

<details>
<summary>Tool output</summary>

```
HYPERSWITCH_API_KEY=snd_[REDACTED]
NEXT_PUBLIC_HYPERSWITCH_PUBLISHABLE_KEY=pk_snd_[REDACTED]
--- local values, prefix only ---
HYPERSWITCH_API_KEY starts with: snd_xxxx
NEXT_PUBLIC_HYPERSWITCH_PUBLISHABLE_KEY starts with: pk_snd_cxxxx
```

</details>

**Response**

Change it to Config. That one is genuinely public.

The Hyperswitch publishable key authenticates the *browser* to Hyperswitch so the Unified Checkout element can render. It's designed to be shipped to the client — same role as a Stripe `pk_`. There's nothing to protect.

Vercel is nagging for a real reason, though: marking a `NEXT_PUBLIC_` variable as Secret buys you **nothing**. Next inlines every `NEXT_PUBLIC_*` value into the JavaScript bundle at build time, so it's in the page source regardless of how Vercel classifies it. The prefix wins, always. Vercel flags it because a Secret-typed public variable is a false sense of security, not a real one.

**The check that actually matters:** confirm the value you pasted starts with `pk_snd_`, not `snd_`. Your local `.env.local` has them correctly distinct — `snd_…` for `HYPERSWITCH_API_KEY`, `pk_snd_…` for the publishable one. If the secret key ever ended up in the `NEXT_PUBLIC_` variable it would be inlined into every browser's page source, and Vercel would show you exactly this warning while it happened.

How to classify each:

| Variable | Type |
|---|---|
| `NEXT_PUBLIC_HYPERSWITCH_PUBLISHABLE_KEY` | Config |
| `NEXT_PUBLIC_APP_URL` | Config |
| `HYPERSWITCH_BASE_URL` | Config — it's the public sandbox URL |
| `HYPERSWITCH_PROFILE_ID` | Either; it's an identifier, not a credential |
| `HYPERSWITCH_API_KEY` | **Secret** |
| `HYPERSWITCH_WEBHOOK_SECRET` | **Secret** |
| `AFTERCARE_SESSION_SECRET` | **Secret** |

The three Secrets are the ones where the classification does real work, because none of them carry the public prefix and none reach the browser.

### 6. Prompt

how do i change to config

**Response**

I can't see your dashboard, and Vercel moves this control around between UI versions, so here's the path plus the fallback.

**In place:** Settings → Environment Variables → find `NEXT_PUBLIC_HYPERSWITCH_PUBLISHABLE_KEY` → the **⋯** menu at the right of the row → **Edit**. In the edit panel there's a type control — a Secret/Config toggle or dropdown, usually next to the value field or under an "Advanced" disclosure. Switch it to Config and save.

**If the type control is greyed out or absent**, that's expected rather than you missing it: Vercel does not allow converting an existing Sensitive/Secret variable to plaintext, because the stored value is write-only once encrypted that way. Delete the variable and re-add it:

1. **⋯** → **Delete** on that row
2. **Add New** → key `NEXT_PUBLIC_HYPERSWITCH_PUBLISHABLE_KEY`, paste the `pk_snd_…` value from `.env.local`
3. Choose **Config** in the type selector on the create form — it's easier to set at creation than to change later
4. Tick Production, Preview, and Development

Either way it needs a redeploy afterward to take effect, since it's inlined at build time.

Worth saying: this is cosmetic. Leaving it marked Secret doesn't break anything — the key still reaches the browser and checkout still works, because the `NEXT_PUBLIC_` prefix controls that, not Vercel's label. So if the UI fights you, it's fine to leave it and move on to the redeploy. The classification only does real work on the three variables that have no public prefix.

**Response**

That's the case I flagged — once a variable is stored as Secret, Vercel encrypts it write-only and the Config option renders but stays disabled. It's not you missing a step; it can't be converted.

Delete and recreate:

1. **⋯** → **Delete** on `NEXT_PUBLIC_HYPERSWITCH_PUBLISHABLE_KEY`
2. **Add New**, same key name
3. Pick **Config** *before* pasting the value — the selector is only live on the create form
4. Paste the `pk_snd_…` value from `.env.local`
5. Tick Production, Preview, Development
6. Redeploy

Or skip it entirely. This is cosmetic — the key reaches the browser either way because the `NEXT_PUBLIC_` prefix decides that, not Vercel's label. Nothing is broken or less secure if you leave it as Secret. The only cost is that Vercel keeps showing the warning, and the value stays hidden from you in the dashboard.

Given you're mid-deploy, my call: leave it, get the redeploy green, come back to it if it bothers you. The three variables where the Secret classification does real work — `HYPERSWITCH_API_KEY`, `HYPERSWITCH_WEBHOOK_SECRET`, `AFTERCARE_SESSION_SECRET` — are already right.

Fixed and green. Two things now, one yours and one mine.

**Yours: `NEXT_PUBLIC_APP_URL` on Vercel.** Check its current value — I'd bet it's `http://localhost:3000` from the copy-paste. Set it to your production URL (no trailing slash, `https://`), then redeploy **without build cache**. That variable is inlined into the client bundle at build time, so a cached build reuses the old value.

**Mine: the fallback is gone.** `/pay` now reads `serverEnv().appUrl`, which is validated and has no default. If the variable is missing, the page fails with a readable error before a payment can be attempted, rather than quietly routing patients to a machine that isn't theirs.

Logged as D-014, because it's worth more than a quiet fix. That fallback was written for local convenience and worked perfectly locally, which is exactly why it survived to production. A default for the app's own public URL can't be correct anywhere except the one machine it names — it can only convert a loud misconfiguration into a silent one.

And the failure shape is the bad one for payments: **the charge succeeded and the confirmation was lost.** The patient doesn't know whether they paid. That's a support call about a payment that actually worked.

Which is the useful part for your writeup: no money was harmed. `pay_mm7pB87qWfz9tSA8BMN4` is `succeeded` at Hyperswitch regardless of where the browser landed. That's precisely why D-006 has `/pay/return` refusing to assert success, and why step 5 makes the webhook the thing that records state. The redirect broke and the design absorbed it. You couldn't stage a better demonstration of the principle than accidentally hitting it.

### 10. Prompt

so what sould i paste into there

**Response**

The production domain, with the scheme and nothing else:

```
https://aftercare-payments.vercel.app
```

That's the likely one given your repo name, but **check it** rather than trusting my guess — Vercel appends a suffix if the name was taken. Project → **Domains** (on the overview, or Settings → Domains). Use the one labelled as the production domain.

Rules for the value:

- `https://`, not `http://`
- **No trailing slash** — the code appends `/pay/return`, so a slash gives you `//pay/return`
- No path

**Don't use the deployment-specific URL.** Vercel shows two kinds: the stable production domain above, and a per-deployment one like `aftercare-payments-k3n8fq2x-yun.vercel.app` that changes on every single deploy. If you paste that one, the return URL breaks again on your next push.

## One correction to what I told you earlier

I said tick all three environments for everything. For this variable specifically, don't — it needs different values per environment:

| Environment | Value |
|---|---|
| Production | `https://aftercare-payments.vercel.app` |
| Preview | `https://aftercare-payments.vercel.app` |
| Development | `http://localhost:3000` |

Vercel lets you set a different value per environment for the same key. If you give Development the production URL, then `vercel env pull` drags it into your local `.env.local` and local dev starts redirecting you to the deployed site mid-payment.

Preview pointing at the production domain isn't strictly right — preview deploys have their own URLs — but it's harmless here and not worth solving for a prototype.

Everything else keeps the same value across all three.

Tree is green — typecheck, lint, 44 tests across 5 files, production build clean. Everything from this session is ready but uncommitted.

**One blocker left: Upstash isn't connected.** The statement flow now reads its ledger from Redis (D-013), so `/api/statements/lookup` and `/statement/[ref]` will fail with a missing-variable error — locally and deployed — until it exists. `/pay` is unaffected, which is why your smoke test still works.

Two steps:

1. **Vercel → Storage → Marketplace → Upstash Redis → connect to this project.** It sets `UPSTASH_REDIS_REST_URL` and `UPSTASH_REDIS_REST_TOKEN` on Vercel itself.
2. **Paste those two into your local `.env.local`** so I can verify the flow before it ships. Find them in the Upstash console under your database → REST API, or in Vercel's env list once the integration adds them. They're already documented in `.env.example`.

Then I'll run the full guest-lookup path locally against real Redis and confirm the balance derivation survives the round trip, which is the part I haven't yet proven — every test so far stubs the store out.

I've held off, since you haven't asked. Ready when you are, as three commits:

1. Exporter `--project`/`--session`/`--from` flags, plus the tool-result prompt-counting fix (D-010, D-011)
2. Step 3 — fixtures, balance derivation, guest lookup, statement render (D-012)
3. Redis-backed ledger and the return-URL fallback removal (D-013, D-014)

My suggestion is to connect Upstash and let me verify first, then commit. That way the first deploy carrying the statement flow comes up green instead of 500ing on a service that isn't wired yet. But if you'd rather bank the work now, say so and I'll commit — nothing in the tree is broken, it just needs a service it doesn't have.

**Response**

Good news: nothing to add on Vercel. The integration already set what the code now reads.

`KV_REST_API_URL` and `KV_REST_API_TOKEN` are the two that matter. The others you can ignore:

- `KV_REST_API_READ_ONLY_TOKEN` — read-only, and this store appends
- `KV_URL` / `REDIS_URL` — TCP connection strings for a socket client. We use the REST client, which is the right shape for serverless: a function that gets frozen between invocations can't hold a socket open

**For local dev**, add these two lines to `.env.local` yourself — don't paste the values into this chat:

```
KV_REST_API_URL=...
KV_REST_API_TOKEN=...
```

Get them from the Upstash console → your database → **REST API**, or reveal them in Vercel's env list. Use the read-write token, not the read-only one.

Then say the word and I'll run the guest lookup end to end against real Redis. That's the piece I still haven't proven — every test so far stubs the store out, so the round trip through Redis and back into balance derivation is currently unverified. I'd rather catch a serialization problem on your machine than on the deployment.

**Response**

Verified end to end against real Redis, then cleaned up.

| Step | Result |
|---|---|
| Lookup with empty ledger | `$32.70` remaining, `open` |
| After a `$20.00` succeeded payment | `$12.70` remaining, still `open` |
| After a second `$12.70` payment | `$0.00` remaining, `paid` |
| Statement page | renders "Paid to date $32.70", "Remaining balance $0.00" |
| Cleanup | ledger empty, statement back to `$32.70` open |

That closes the gap I flagged: every test stubs the store out, so the JSON round trip through Upstash and back into `deriveBalance` was unproven until now. It works, and the quotes Vercel displays around values are stripped by Next's dotenv parser as expected.

Worth noting what it also demonstrates: two payment records against one statement, neither of which pays it off alone, resolving to `paid` only when the sum matches. That's the split tender mechanic from `DESIGN.md` §8 working before step 6 formally builds it — the derivation was written for it, so it came free.

What this does **not** prove: that `/api/payments/intent` creates a real payment against a statement (step 4), or that a webhook writes one (step 5). Those are still ahead.

# Hyperswitch Webhook Signature Verification Details

## HTTP Header & Algorithm
The primary signature header is `x-webhook-signature-512`, which uses the "HMAC-SHA512" algorithm. A fallback option exists: `x-webhook-signature-256` with "HMAC-SHA256" for systems lacking SHA-512 support.

## Merchant Secret
The signature is computed using the `payment_response_hash_key` field, which merchants can specify when creating a business profile. If not provided, "a 64-character long randomized key with high entropy will be generated for you."

Docs reviewed against the brief. Here's where everything stands.

## Session review: what you'd actually read

| | Size |
|---|---|
| Design session, trimmed from prompt 2 | 16 prompts, 4,327 lines (~150 KB) |
| This session | 14 prompts, still growing |
| `ai-sessions/README.md` index | stub — not written |

The 4,327 lines sounds worse than it is: most of it is collapsed tool output inside `<details>` blocks. The 16 prompts are what you're actually reviewing, and I've already swept it for credentials (none) and personal material (one contiguous block, cut at the prompt-1 boundary).

The index is the part that isn't done, and `HANDOFF.md` is blunt that **the index is the deliverable** — nobody reads a raw transcript. Three or four pointers to specific moments. There are good candidates now: the model asserting a documented API path that disagreed with another documented path (D-007), the in-memory state assumption that building falsified (D-013), and the localhost fallback that shipped a successful payment to a dead redirect (D-014).

## Against the assignment

**Deliverable 1 — 3-page architecture doc.** Not started, and it's explicitly self-written; `HANDOFF.md` forbids me producing it. The raw material is all in `DOMAIN.md`, `DESIGN.md`, `SCOPE.md`, `DECISIONS.md`. This is your largest remaining item.

**Deliverable 2 — AI session.** Export ready, index unwritten.

**Deliverable 3 — working prototype, hosted.** Deployed, but the brief's core sentence is *"takes a user from the purchase journey through to a real, completed payment."* Today the journey works (lookup → bill explanation) and a real payment works (`/pay`), but **they are not connected.** Build step 4 joins them, and until it does, the single most important requirement of the assignment is unmet. Steps 4–8 remain.

## The thing I'd fix before anything else

`docs/SCOPE.md` has a "Built" table listing nine flows. **Two are actually built.** It currently claims webhook ingestion, split tender, partial refunds, ACH, decline handling, and health account classification are done. They aren't.

It was written as a plan and reads as a claim. Submitted today, a payments engineer would check one of those against the repo, find nothing, and stop trusting the rest of the document — which would be a shame, because the deferral analysis under it is the strongest writing in the repo. It becomes true around step 6. Until then it needs splitting into built and planned, or a status column.

## Hyperswitch doc findings

**Webhooks confirmed exactly.** `x-webhook-signature-512`, HMAC-SHA512, keyed on `payment_response_hash_key`. Two additions worth having before step 5: the `event_id` dedup window is documented as **24 hours**, and there are 18 event types, not the 5 in `HANDOFF.md` — including `payment_cancelled`, `payment_authorized`, `payment_captured`, `action_required`, the full dispute lifecycle, and mandate events.

**Step 6 has the data it needs.** Card responses carry `card_isin` (6-digit BIN), `card_extended_bin` (8), plus `card_network`, `card_issuer`, `card_type`, `last4` — which maps cleanly onto `TenderDetail` in `types.ts`. The HSA/FSA classification argument in `DOMAIN.md` §5 holds up: it really is a BIN lookup, not an integration.

**`HANDOFF.md` step 7 was wrong, and I've corrected it.** The blocklist is API-only, not dashboard-configured, and there's no separate card-testing guard — the blocklist toggle *is* the guard. Endpoints are `POST /blocklist/toggle?status=true`, `POST /blocklist`, `GET /blocklist?data_kind=payment_method`, `DELETE /blocklist`; blocked payments fail `HE_03`. That's uncommitted.

Sources: [Webhooks](https://docs.hyperswitch.io/integration-guide/webhooks.md), [Fraud Blocklist](https://docs.hyperswitch.io/integration-guide/workflows/fraud-and-risk-management/fraud-blocklist), [Payment processor capabilities](https://docs.hyperswitch.io/integrations/connectors-integrations/payment-processor-capabilities)

Want me to start step 4 — paying a real statement — while you review the session export? That's the one that turns this into what the brief actually asked for.

**Response**

This is the useful part — and it changes my recommendation. The tools `DESIGN-SYSTEM.md` isn't a brand doc, it's a *method*, and it's rigorous: contrast measured rather than guessed (`.35 measured 2.98 and failed its own floor`), gold given different hexes per ground because `#B5936A` on cream is 2.6:1, terracotta reserved so it "means load or warning only, never decorative."

And one rule in it maps onto this app almost too neatly:

> Dark reads as instrument; cream reads as document. Anything you *manipulate or read off* goes in the hero. Anything you *read* goes in the body.

A medical statement **is** a document. A payment **is** an instrument. That's a real design thesis for Aftercare rather than a coat of paint, and it's the opposite of boilerplate — the current pages are one undifferentiated grey ground, which is exactly why they read as generic.

Where I'd push back: the YUNVO *brand* is fitness DTC. Barlow Condensed 900 uppercase, gold CTAs, crane-and-tiger, "where strength meets wisdom" — on a $927 medical bill from "Northgate Health Partners" that's incoherent, and a Juspay reviewer would read it as confusion about the exercise rather than as design confidence.

[422]

--- dollar float (expect 400) ---
{"paymentId":"9c96f855-6667-4014-805e-5626ae1f8782","hyperswitchPaymentId":"pay_c3llJTfrzDpagP7wgGGu","clientSecret":"pay_c3llJTfrzDpagP7wgGGu_secret_S55qd23k2LCv2NL1BODq","amount":927,"currency":"USD"}

```

</details>

**Response**

Found a real bug, and it's the exact class the `Cents` type exists to prevent.

`{"amount": 927.00}` was **accepted and charged $9.27** instead of $927. JSON parses `927.00` to the integer `927`, so `Number.isInteger` passes and no type check can catch it. A patient-side dollar/cents mixup becomes a silent hundred-fold underpayment.

The fix isn't better validation — it's removing the free-form number entirely.

**Response**

Step 4 is done and the gate passed. Here's the architecture review.

## What's holding up

**The layering is doing real work.** `types.ts` → `money` → `balance` → `intent` is pure: no I/O, no clock, no network. 57 tests run against it with nothing mocked except the store. `store.ts` is the only I/O boundary, routes are thin, pages read. That's why the Redis swap in D-013 was a module change rather than a rewrite.

**Derived status paid for itself twice.** Split tender needed no code — two payment records against one statement already resolve correctly, because status is a fold rather than a field. And when D-017 turned up, the fix was a fold rule, not a migration.

**Amount authority is now structural rather than procedural.** After D-015 there is no number in the request to validate. That's a stronger guarantee than the one `HANDOFF.md` invariant 6 asked for.

## What I'd change, in order

**1. Intent creation isn't idempotent.** Every POST creates a real Hyperswitch payment and a ledger row. I created six real sandbox payments just testing this hour. A patient double-clicking, or React remounting the checkout, does the same — and each orphan sits in `payment_pending` until a webhook resolves it. Fix before step 6: reuse an in-flight intent for the same statement and portion, or take an idempotency key.

**2. No index from processor payment id to statement.** Step 5 receives a Hyperswitch id and the ledger is keyed by statement, so there's no way to find the statement without scanning. The payload echoes `metadata.statement_ref`, but resolving our own ledger through a field the processor round-tripped is fragile. Briefed into `HANDOFF.md` — write the index at intent time.

**3. `store.ts` holds two different things.** Static fixtures (synchronous, immutable, would come from a practice management system) and the mutable ledger (async, would be a real datastore). Different lifetimes, different failure modes, different production substitutes. Worth splitting.

**4. `types.ts` has drifted.** `CreateIntentRequest.amount` no longer describes the boundary after D-015. You own that contract, so I left it and flagged it in the decision entry rather than editing it.

## What building falsified

Three design assumptions, which is the part worth putting in your architecture doc:

- **§12, "in-memory fixtures, no database."** False the moment a route handler and a page needed to share state (D-013).
- **§13, `GET /api/statements/:ref`.** A GET can't carry a date of birth (D-012).
- **§8 and invariant 6, "validate the client-supplied amount."** The implementation followed that rule exactly and still charged $9.27 for a $927 bill (D-015).

Each of those was sound reasoning about the case it considered and wrong about the case it didn't. That's a better story than a design that survived contact unchanged.

## Design

The two-ground split is in: instrument (dark, grotesque) for the balance and the act of paying, document (cream, serif) for the adjudication. Radius zero throughout, semantic-only colour, contrast measured and recorded in `docs/DESIGN-SYSTEM.md`. The `/pay` smoke test page is gone — once the intent route is statement-bound, an unauthenticated payment path is a hole.

The accent you had, `#1f5f4f`, measures 2.58:1 on a dark ground. That's why the old version's dark areas looked muddy. It now has two hexes, one per ground.

---

Uncommitted: step 4, the design system, D-015 through D-017, the SCOPE status column, and the step 5 briefing. 57 tests, build clean. Want me to commit — and shall I take step 5 next, or would you rather review the session export first?

**Response**

Step 5 is done and committed. The gate passed against the live application with real signatures:

```
3. unsigned webhook            401, balance unchanged
4. verified payment_succeeded  applied, remaining 0, status paid
5. replay, same event_id       duplicate, balance unchanged
6. older updated, failed       stale, balance did not walk back
```

**It failed on the first run, and the reason was worth finding.** A correctly signed `payment_succeeded` was rejected as stale, so the statement stayed unpaid. The intent route stamped its record with `new Date()` — our wall clock — while the webhook carries Hyperswitch's `updated`. Two clocks compared as one. `types.ts` says that field is "processor timestamp from the webhook payload, not our clock"; the contract was right and the implementation hadn't read it closely enough.

No unit test could have caught it. Every ordering test builds both records itself and is internally consistent by construction — the mismatch only exists where our clock meets theirs, which is exactly the boundary a fixture replaces. Logged as D-018.

Fixed by confirming against a live sandbox payment rather than assuming: the create response does return `updated`, alongside `created`, `modified_at`, `expires_on`. The intent record now stores it, so ordering compares two readings of one clock.

Payload field names came from the Hyperswitch source, not inference — `event_id`, `event_type`, `content.type`, `content.object`. One real inconsistency to know about: **payments carry their ordering timestamp as `updated`, refunds as `updated_at`.** That's in the API itself; the parser reads both.

Also caught in passing: the gate reported everything as duplicate on one run because my script reused fixed event ids and the claim holds 24 hours. That was deduplication working correctly on a test that was wrong — the failure mode that looks most like a bug and isn't.

**Where the build stands:** steps 1–5 done, 79 tests, build clean. Steps 6 (health account BIN classification, split tender, partial refund, decline handling), 7 (risk controls), 8 (cold test) remain.

**Two things on your side before the webhook path works in production:**

1. Register the endpoint — Hyperswitch dashboard → Developers → Payment Settings → Webhook Setup → `https://<your-vercel-url>/api/webhooks/hyperswitch`
2. Push and redeploy. Nothing from today is pushed yet, and the deploy still needs Upstash connected.

Want me to take step 6 next, or would you rather push and confirm the webhook fires against the real deployment first? I'd lean toward the latter — step 6's refund flow is easier to build once real webhooks are landing.

The evidence says a real git-linked deployment does exist — you paid a live sandbox payment through it, the env vars applied, and `/pay/return` rendered our code including the D-006 wording. That's our repo running on Vercel. So the project is there; it's just a matter of finding its domain.

### 21. Prompt

i changed the url but uhh what is the session secret from

**Response**

It's ours, not Hyperswitch's or Vercel's — nothing to look up.

It signs the guest access cookie. When a patient passes the statement-reference-plus-date-of-birth lookup, the server issues an HMAC-SHA256 token over the statement id and an expiry, and that cookie is what lets the statement page render without the date of birth travelling again. The secret is the HMAC key. It exists because holding those grants in memory failed — a route handler and a page get different module instances (D-013).

**Where the value comes from: you generate it.** Any 32 bytes of randomness. It doesn't need to match anything, and it shouldn't match your local one:

```bash
node -e "console.log(require('node:crypto').randomBytes(32).toString('hex'))"
```

Run that, copy the output, paste it into Vercel as `AFTERCARE_SESSION_SECRET`, type Secret, all three environments.

Two notes:

**Don't paste the value here.** This session is being shared as an interview deliverable. The exporter redacts things shaped like `snd_`, `sk_test_`, `whsec_` and so on, but a bare hex string matches none of those patterns and would go out in the transcript.

**Changing it later is safe.** The only effect is that access cookies issued under the old key stop verifying, so anyone mid-session looks their statement up again. No payment or ledger data is keyed to it.

Separately — I already put a generated value in your local `.env.local`, which is gitignored, so local dev already works. It's only Vercel that's missing one.

I'm watching the production ledger now — it polls every six seconds for fifteen minutes and logs every state change.

Go make the payment:

1. **https://aftercare-payments.vercel.app/**

What I expect to see, and what each part proves:

| Transition | What it means |
|---|---|
| `open` → `payment_pending` | The intent row landed. No money counted yet — `deriveBalance` only counts succeeded |
| `payment_pending` → `paid`, `remaining=0` | A **genuine Hyperswitch webhook**, signature-verified, moved it |

The second one is the whole point. Every webhook I've tested so far I signed myself, so this is the first time the real processor's signature is checked against the real secret. If the header encoding differs from the hex I assumed, this is where it shows up — and the log will say `length=88 hex=false` rather than failing silently.

Also worth watching: if you close the tab immediately after confirming, the statement should still reach `paid`. The redirect is a hint; the webhook is the truth. That's the design claim from `DOMAIN.md` and it's never been tested against a real delivery.

**The real webhook landed and it worked.** This is the claim the whole design rests on, and it's now proven end to end:

```
pay_hz3mLO8GL41QrB5AgEN1  requires_payment_method  updatedAt 23:23:56   (intent row)
pay_hz3mLO8GL41QrB5AgEN1  succeeded                updatedAt 23:24:11   (webhook row)
                                                    tender: Visa •4242

statement: remaining 0, status paid
```

Four things that were assumptions until fifteen seconds ago: the hex encoding of the HMAC-SHA512 signature was right, the correlation index resolved a real processor id to the right statement, the D-017 fold counted two rows as one $32.70 payment rather than $65.40, and the D-018 clock fix let a genuine `updated` compare correctly. Tender came off the payload too.

Now your three points.

**Northgate Health Partners is mine — I invented it.** It's the fixture provider name in `fixtures.ts`, and it doubles as the card statement descriptor, which is why it's `NORTHGATE HEALTH` in caps there. Nothing real. It exists because a bill needs a provider on it and the descriptor argument in `DESIGN.md` §10 needs something concrete. Happy to rename it to anything you prefer — say the word.

**The date of birth field: you're right, and a calendar would be worse.** Native `<input type="date">` gives you that `mm/dd/yyyy` segmented thing that fights you. But a date picker is actively wrong for a birth date — you'd page back sixty years through a calendar built for picking next Tuesday. Dropdowns aren't great either; a year select is a hundred-item list.

The researched pattern for dates a person knows from memory is three separate short text fields. That's what GOV.UK's design system uses for exactly this case. I'll build that.

**The receipt is a real gap, and it's already specified.** `DESIGN.md` §14 says the return page should poll with bounded backoff and never claim success it can't verify — right now it just reports the redirect and stops. And §4 has a receipt in the flow diagram. So the honest version is: return page polls until the webhook lands, then shows a real receipt with what was paid, when, and on what tender.

Let me build all three.

**Response**

Committed. All three addressed.

**The date of birth is now three fields** — `MM / DD / YYYY`, numeric keyboards, autofill hints (`bday-month` and friends), focus advancing only when a field is unambiguously full so typing `1` for January doesn't throw you into the next box before you can type the `2` of `12`.

It also validates in the browser now, which matters more than it looks. The server deliberately returns the same vague "no statement matches that reference and date of birth" for a typo and for a wrong record (D-012, so the endpoint isn't an oracle for valid references). Without client-side checking, someone who typed month 13 gets that message and has no way to tell which mistake they made. `31 April` is rejected rather than silently rolled into `1 May`, which is what `Date` does with it.

**The receipt is built**, and it renders from your actual payment:

```
Paid in full · Statement AFT-4021-8837
Visa ending 4242            $32.70
Reference pay_hz3mLO8GL41QrB5AgEN1
Your responsibility         $32.70
Paid                        $32.70
Balance                      $0.00
```

It uses the same fold as the balance, so a receipt can't disagree with what the patient was charged. It also names the card descriptor (`NORTHGATE HEALTH`) — the cheapest chargeback prevention there is, since disputes start with someone not recognising a line on their statement.

**The return page now does what `DESIGN.md` §14 always specified** and never did: polls the derived status on a bounded schedule, hands over the receipt once the ledger moves. On your payment that gap was fifteen seconds. After ~30s it stops and says the payment is still confirming, that this is normal, that it's safe to close the page and not to pay again — claiming neither success nor failure, because at that point it knows neither.

**On Northgate Health Partners** — it's mine, invented for the fixtures, and it doubles as the card descriptor. What would you rather it be? It's a one-line change in `fixtures.ts`.

Two things worth noting:

I couldn't screenshot — the browser pane stopped painting (it's probably behind your window). I verified everything by HTML and the accessibility tree instead. The dev server is running on `localhost:3000` if you want to look.

Your paid statement is real data, so I left it. `AFT-4021-8837` will show `paid` with a receipt; the other two are clean.

Want me to push? That's two commits now — this one and step 5's follow-ups — and pushing redeploys automatically.

## On colour-coding the receipt

Worth doing, with one constraint: the system we just built says colour is **only ever semantic** — terracotta means "your plan did not cover this," green means "money collected," and neither is available for emphasis. So the question isn't "where can we add colour," it's "what distinctions does a receipt actually make."

There are three, and only one is currently encoded:

| State | Now | Should be |
|---|---|---|
| Money collected | green ✓ | green |
| Money returned to the patient | **no treatment** | its own hue — a refund is not a payment, and reading it as one is how a patient thinks they paid twice |
| Still owed | **no treatment** | the residual, carried by weight and size rather than a fourth colour |

The refund case is the real gap. Right now a `+$50.00` refund row and a `$32.70` payment row look identical apart from the sign, and direction of money is exactly the thing a receipt exists to make unambiguous. I'd add one token for "returned," deliberately off the paid/warn ladder — the parent system does the same thing with its "better-than" green, which it keeps off the severity scale on purpose.

What I'd resist: colouring "still owed" terracotta. That colour already means "your plan didn't cover this line," and overloading it makes both meanings weaker.

## What I'd reconsider or rebuild

Ranked by what a payments reviewer would actually poke at.

**1. Health account BIN classification is not built, and it's the whole differentiator.** `tenderFrom()` in the webhook handler hardcodes `standard_card` with a comment saying step 6 will fix it. But the webhook already delivers `card_isin` — the BIN — and we're throwing it away. Right now this is a well-built generic payment flow; HSA/FSA recognition is the part that makes it *healthcare*. It's also the cheapest big win left, because the data is already arriving.

**2. Intent creation still isn't idempotent.** Every POST creates a real Hyperswitch payment and a ledger row. I've created roughly ten in testing today. A patient double-clicking does the same, and each orphan sits in `payment_pending`. This is a real defect, not a nicety.

**3. Decline handling has no path.** `DOMAIN.md` §6 calls this the highest-value error path in the vertical, and `SCOPE.md` lists it as core. If a card fails, the patient currently sees the SDK's inline error and nothing else — no offer of a different method, no record they can see.

**4. Split tender has an API and no interface.** `portion: "health_account"` works and is tested. Nothing in the UI lets a patient choose it. The one flow that most needs demonstrating is reachable only by curl.

**5. `store.ts` holds two different things**, and the lookup route still throws an unhandled 500 with no message if the Upstash credentials go missing — every other route names the variable.

**6. Deliverable-facing, and cheap:** `README.md` still says "Design complete. Implementation in progress," doesn't link the deployed URL, and `ai-sessions/README.md` is still the stub. That index is a graded deliverable and currently has nothing in it.

**7. Never tested on a phone.** Step 8. The tables scroll inside their own container, but the real flow on a small screen is unknown.

My recommendation: **1, then 3 and 4 together** — they're all step 6, they're what `SCOPE.md` claims is core, and they're what turns this from a competent integration into a domain argument. Then 2, then the deliverable cleanup.

Want me to take the receipt colour and the health account classification together? They're both small, and the classification makes the receipt more interesting — a receipt that says "Health account card ending 4242" is doing real work that a generic one isn't.

Both built and pushed. Deploying now.

**Health account recognition is live.** The webhook was already delivering `card_isin` and the handler was discarding it. Now it classifies, and this is the part of the prototype that's specific to *healthcare* rather than to payments generally.

Verified end to end with signed webhooks on the mixed-eligibility statement:

```
health_account portion   $702.00 paid → remaining $225.00, open
full portion             $225.00 paid → remaining   $0.00, paid

receipt:  Health account card ending 4444    $702.00
          Visa ending 4242                   $225.00
```

That's `DESIGN.md` §8's split tender scenario actually running.

**The BIN table is deliberately half-empty.** The authoritative health-benefit IIN ranges are licensed to SIGIS-registered merchants and aren't public, so the licensed set is empty rather than filled with plausible-looking numbers — an invented range that looks real is worse than none, because it invites someone to trust it. The demo set holds sandbox test BINs, labelled as such.

Unrecognised BINs fail towards `standard_card`. Calling a health account card standard costs some tailored handling; calling a standard card a health account tells a patient funds are eligible when they aren't and would route a refund on a false premise. Only one of those has a tax consequence.

**Refunds got their own colour** — `#35617f` on cream at 5.93:1, `#8ec0dc` on the instrument at 9.86:1. Off the paid ladder deliberately: a refund in the same green as a payment differs only by a plus sign, and direction of money is the thing a receipt exists to disambiguate. I left the outstanding balance uncoloured — it's already the biggest number on the page, and a fourth colour would dilute the three that mean something.

## To review

Once the deploy lands (~40s), pay any statement with **`5555 5555 5555 4444`** instead of the Visa test card. The receipt will say **"Health account card ending 4444"** in the accent colour, plus a note explaining that a refund has to return to that same account.

`AFT-4021-8837` is already paid from your real payment earlier — its receipt shows `Visa ending 4242`, so you can compare the two treatments directly.

One honest gap: **the split itself has no interface.** `portion: "health_account"` works and is tested, but nothing in the UI lets a patient choose "pay the $702 my FSA covers." I drove it by curl. That's the natural next piece, and it's what makes the classification visible rather than just correct — a checkout that says *"your health account can cover $702.00 of this"* and offers that as a button.

Pushed and deploying.

**The split tender flow now exists in the interface.** On `AFT-4108-2290` ($927.00, $702.00 eligible):

```
Amount due
$927.00

$702.00 of this is eligible for HSA or FSA funds. The rest is not,
so a health account card cannot cover all of it.

  [ Pay $702.00 with a health account card ]
  [ Pay the full $927.00 with one method   ]

Paying the eligible part first leaves $225.00 to settle with another method.
Nothing is charged until you enter a card.
```

On `AFT-3994-1177`, where every line is eligible, it skips straight to checkout — an option with one sensible answer is a question, not a choice.

The point isn't the buttons. Without this, someone enters an FSA card against the full balance and gets declined for ineligible spend, with a processor code that explains nothing. The eligible figure is knowable before the card is entered, so it's shown before the card is entered.

**One thing I tightened while building it.** The panel computes the eligible figure to *display* and the server computes it again to *charge*. Same rule, same statement, but two derivations — and two derivations of one quantity can drift. Checkout now renders the amount from the intent response, so the patient sees the number the server actually created the payment for, before confirming rather than on the receipt.

## To review

Pay `AFT-4108-2290` (DOB `1975-11-02`) with **`5555 5555 5555 4444`**, choosing the $702.00 option. You should get: a receipt naming "Health account card ending 4444," the statement still open at $225.00, and a "Pay the remaining $225.00" button. Settling that with the Visa test card completes the split.

## What's still open

From the review list, in the order I'd take them:

1. **Intent idempotency** — now more visible, since "Change the amount" abandons an intent by design. Recorded in D-023 rather than hidden, but it's a real defect.
2. **Decline handling** — `DOMAIN.md` calls it the highest-value error path in this vertical and there's still no path for it.
3. **Partial refund after re-adjudication** — the last unbuilt core flow from `SCOPE.md`.
4. **`README.md` and the `ai-sessions/` index** — both stale, both deliverable-facing, both cheap.

Say which and I'll take it.

**Response**

Decline handling is in and pushed. 105 tests.

**The domain-specific part is that one code means two things.** Verified against the running app — identical `error_code`, two classifications:

```
insufficient_funds on a personal card    → insufficient_funds
insufficient_funds on a health account   → health_account_limit
```

A personal card declining for funds might work tomorrow. A health account declining for funds means the balance is smaller than the bill — the ordinary case, not a fault — and the answer is to pay the eligible portion and settle the rest another way. Telling that patient "try again later" is advice that cannot work.

**Two rules on the wording, both tested.** It never implies the patient did something wrong, because a bill arriving weeks after the care is already an unpleasant surprise and a decline reads as an accusation if you let it. And it always says what to do next — including when it recognises nothing at all, because "your card was declined" with no suggestion is exactly where collection stops.

What the pay page shows now:

> **Your bank did not approve this payment**
> This is usually a limit or a balance on the bank's side rather than anything wrong with the card. Another card or a bank account will often work, and you can also pay part of the balance now and the rest later.
> Nothing was charged, and the balance below is unchanged.

**Two design calls worth knowing about:**

The ledger stores the *category*, not the sentence. Connector codes are connector-specific and change, and showing one to a patient is what this normalization exists to prevent. Storing the category means the wording can improve without rewriting history.

`unified_code` / `unified_message` — Hyperswitch's cross-connector normalization, and exactly what this module would rather consume — are marked **"not live yet"** in the source. I read them when present and expect null, with a local mapping doing the work until they ship. Most of `decline.ts` is deletable when they do. That's a genuine point in the orchestration layer's favour for your writeup: the abstraction is specified, just not delivered.

The decline is read from the ledger rather than a query parameter, because a redirect can be lost and the ledger can't. And the return page stops polling the instant a failure is recorded, instead of making someone watch a spinner for thirty seconds after their card was already refused.

The real refund webhook **did** arrive and succeeded — but the balance ignored it. That's a bug, and a good one.

```
payments:  internal=4adb2938…  hs=pay_hz3m…  requires_payment_method   (intent row)
           internal=6760b715…  hs=pay_hz3m…  succeeded                 (webhook row)

```

Two rows describe one processor payment, and the refund got bound to different ones. `deriveBalance` builds its id set from the *folded* payments, so the succeeded refund pointed at a row that had been folded away and was silently dropped.

**Response**

The refund flow is built and **proven with a real Hyperswitch refund** — not a simulation.

```
correction:  $32.70 → $20.00 responsibility
refund:      ref_gmey0mFKKTTx5cRWbhBa, $12.70 to pay_hz3mLO8GL41QrB5AgEN1
webhook:     refund_succeeded, verified, applied
statement:   partially_refunded, remaining $0.00
```

Your receipt now reads:

```
Corrected, and settled.
Visa ending 4242                              $32.70
Refunded to your original payment method     +$12.70
Your responsibility                           $20.00
Paid                                          $32.70
Returned to you                              -$12.70
Balance                                        $0.00

Your insurer reprocessed this claim on September 4, 2026…
```

## Doing it for real found two bugs, and they were the same bug

The genuine `refund_succeeded` webhook arrived, verified, was recorded — **and the balance ignored it.** The statement kept saying `paid` while $12.70 had actually left the provider.

The log holds several rows per processor payment. The intent route writes one, each webhook writes another, and a refund binds to whichever row its writer happened to hold — the webhook held the *intent* row, `readjudicate` held the *settled* one. Both `deriveBalance` and `settledActivity` built their payment-id sets from the **folded** result, so a refund attached to a discarded row matched nothing and vanished.

It was invisible to every test because a fixture author naturally binds a refund to the payment they just wrote. The disagreement only exists when two pieces of code write rows for the same payment at different times — which is exactly what an append-only log does and exactly what a fixture doesn't. Both regression tests now construct that disagreement deliberately.

**That's the second bug of this shape after D-017.** Both silent, both money, both from one part of the code holding a different view of the log than another. Worth putting in your architecture doc: append-only means the read side carries the interpretation, and every reader must fold identically.

## Where the design earned out

The hard question — which payment do you refund on a split-tender balance — was already answered in `DESIGN.md` §9 before any code existed: **health account last**, because returning money to a personal card is unambiguously fine while returning it to a health account is a reversal against a tax-advantaged account. A $225 correction on a $702 FSA + $225 Visa statement comes entirely off the Visa. Fourteen tests hold that.

And the destination constraint is satisfied *by construction* rather than by a remembered rule: Hyperswitch refunds reference a `payment_id`, so the money physically cannot land anywhere else.

## `SCOPE.md` is now honest

Eight of nine core flows are **Built**. The ninth I marked "Connector configured, untested" — ACH is enabled on the connector and no ACH payment has ever gone through this app, which is a different claim from built.

## What's left

- **Intent idempotency** — the one real known defect
- **`README.md` + `ai-sessions/` index** — both stale, both graded, both cheap
- **`/provider` is unauthenticated** — deliberate and recorded in SCOPE, but worth you seeing the page
- **Mobile / cold test** (step 8)

The provider console is at [/provider](https://aftercare-payments.vercel.app/provider) if you want to drive a correction yourself.

**Response**

All four are done and deployed.

**Intent idempotency.** Five consecutive requests now produce one processor payment instead of five. It reuses an existing intent, but asks Hyperswitch whether it's still usable first — our log records what we last heard, the processor knows what's true now, and intents expire on their own about fifteen minutes out. `requires_customer_action` is deliberately excluded: that payment is mid-3DS on the patient's bank page, and handing a second browser the same secret is a race, not a reuse.

**Mobile was genuinely broken**, and measurably so: 375px viewport, 570px document, **195px of horizontal overflow**. The statement table carries a 34rem min-width so its columns stay readable, inside a wrapper with `overflow-x: auto` meant to contain it — except grid children default to `min-width: auto` and refuse to shrink below their content, so the width propagated up through the card and the stack and took the page with it. Now 0px overflow, table scrolling in its own box. (Your own tools `CLAUDE.md` lists this as "trap 1", which is how I knew where to look.)

**README** rewritten — live URL, the fixture table with which card demonstrates what, an accurate built list.

**`ai-sessions/README.md`** written. Four moments, all of them failures found by running against a real processor rather than by reading code: the `927.00` that charged $9.27, the real refund that arrived verified and was silently ignored, the in-memory assumption killed by instrumenting it, and the successful payment sent to a dead redirect. Plus the one place I told you no, on the brand.

## Two things you should know

**I leaked your own data into the transcript, and then fixed it.** While reviewing the design session earlier, my tool output echoed your compensation line and the [REDACTED] prep filenames into *this* session — which was then going to be exported. `--from` couldn't help; they were mid-session.

The exporter now takes `--redact <file>`, a list of literal strings loaded from a **gitignored** file. Committing "redact this employer's name" into a public repo publishes the employer's name. Both exports now sweep clean: no live credentials, no compensation, no other-employer references. 23 redaction markers in this session's file.

**The transcripts are gitignored and won't ship.** `.gitignore:35` has `ai-sessions/session-*.md`, so the index is committed but the files it points at aren't. That predates today.

Deliverable 2 is "share your Claude / AI session," so this needs a decision: either un-ignore them so they travel with the repo and the reviewer can follow the index, or keep them out and send the two files separately. I'd un-ignore — the index is useless without the files, and the whole point of the redaction work was to make them safe to publish.

Want me to remove that line? And the `/provider` console is still unauthenticated by design, recorded in `SCOPE.md` item 10 — worth a look before you decide whether that stays a stated boundary or gets a real login.

## What the sweep already guarantees

- **No live credentials.** Every key-shaped string is `[REDACTED]`.
- **No compensation figures, no former employer, no other-employer prep files.**
- **No other project directories** except this repo and the [REDACTED] one — and that one is needed, since `DECISIONS.md` D-011 already names it in the committed record to explain why the design session's transcript lives elsewhere.

92 redactions applied to this session's file, 17 to the design session's.

## What only you can judge

**Your own prompts, quoted verbatim** — typos and all. `"lets first do some planning and documentation"`, `"where is the juspay stuff folder so i can putin my env secrest"`. I'd keep them exactly as they are; edited prompts read as staged, and the brief asks how you work with AI, not how you type. But they're yours, so read them once.

**Anything you pasted in** — recruiter wording, dashboard values, error text. The review sheet lists every prompt with a line number so you can jump to each.

**The invented names.** Northgate Health Partners, Dana Whitfield, Marcus Oyelaran, Priya Raghunathan appear throughout both transcripts and the codebase. All fictional, but they're the ones a reviewer will see.

One thing worth deciding deliberately: the transcripts show me finding **five separate money bugs** — the $9.27 charge, the ignored refund, the clock mismatch, the double-count, the dead redirect. That's the honest record and `HANDOFF.md` argues it's the strongest version. It also means a reviewer watches the model get things wrong repeatedly. I think that's a feature and the index frames it that way, but you're the one submitting it.

Nothing has shipped. `.gitignore:35` still holds `ai-sessions/session-*.md`, the working tree is clean, and the repo currently has the index committed without the files it points at. Say the word once you've read them and I'll drop that line so they travel with the repo.

## The risk screen

At [`/provider/risk`](https://aftercare-payments.vercel.app/provider/risk). It leads with the exposure rather than burying it: a checkout reachable by a statement reference, no account, any card, small amount, is a **card testing target**. That's not a flaw in the design — it's the price of it, since requiring an account in front of a medical bill is the largest source of abandonment in this vertical. The tradeoff was taken deliberately in `DOMAIN.md` §8; this is where its cost is visible.

**The signal that matters isn't the failure count, it's distinct cards.** Three failures on one card is somebody whose bank keeps saying no. Three failures on three cards is something else. Both are tested, and the screen shows the distinction.

Attempts are counted per processor payment, not per log row — an ordinary payment writes two rows, and counting rows would report every payment as two attempts and make the failure rate meaningless.

The blocklist is read **live** from Hyperswitch, not assumed. If the call fails it reports *unknown*, not *empty* — an operator told "nothing is blocked" when the truth is "we couldn't ask" has been told the opposite of what's useful.

And the controls table lists **four rows as Missing**: rate limiting, velocity limiting, console authentication, risk-conditioned 3DS. That's the point of it. A risk page listing only what's present tells an operator they're covered, which is the failure this kind of screen exists to prevent.

## Calling the API found another documentation gap

```
GET /blocklist?data_kind=fingerprint
400  unknown variant `fingerprint`, expected one of `payment_method`,
     `card_bin`, `extended_card_bin`, `generic_card_bin`
```

The same concept is `fingerprint` when you block it and `payment_method` when you list it. The client carries two unions, which looks like duplication and isn't. `generic_card_bin` appears only in that error message and nowhere in the docs — declared and deliberately unused, because an endpoint accepting a value isn't the same as knowing what it means.

Guard is now **enabled** on the account, with BIN `411111` blocked as a demo entry. Neither test card is in that range, so the flow stays payable.

## Extracted into the repo

`HANDOFF.md`'s environment section listed four outstanding items, all of which are done. It now carries the deployed URL, every env var and its source, the Hyperswitch account state, and the two processor ids that prove the path.

One item answered itself: it asked someone to verify the connector wasn't the dummy one. **A real partial refund settled** — and dummy-connector payments can't be refunded. The flow working was the verification.

Build steps 1–7 complete. 128 tests. What's left is step 8 (cold test on a phone), your 3-page doc, and reading the transcript before it ships.
