### 1. The third missing fold: `alreadyRefunded` sums raw refund rows — REAL BUG, highest impact

**`src/lib/domain/refund.ts:48-54`**

```ts
function alreadyRefunded(payment: Payment, refunds: readonly Refund[]): Cents {
  return cents(
    refunds
      .filter((r) =&gt; r.paymentId === payment.id &amp;&amp; r.status !== "failed")
      .reduce((total, r) =&gt; total + r.amount, 0),
  );
}
```

This is the only remaining money computation that reads the refund log without `latestPerProcessorId`. It is the exact shape of D-017 and D-026, and unlike those it fires on **every single refund**, not in an edge case.

Sequence:
1. `POST /api/provider/readjudicate` on `AFT-4108-2290` ($927.00 paid in full, one card). Revised to $700.00 → `owedBack` $227.00.
2. Route appends a `pending` refund row bound to payment row `S` (`readjudicate/route.ts:171-181`), with `hyperswitchRefundId: ref_x`.
3. `refund_succeeded` webhook arrives. `applyRefund` binds it to `forPayment.find(p =&gt; p.status === "succeeded")` — the same row `S` — and appends a second row, same `ref_x` (`webhooks/hyperswitch/route.ts:145-166`).
4. `aftercare:refunds:S` now holds two rows for one $227.00 refund.
5. `deriveBalance` is fine — it folds on `hyperswitchRefundId` (`balance.ts:146-153`). **`alreadyRefunded` does not.** It reports $454.00 refunded against a $927.00 payment.
6. Payer corrects again, revised to $0.00. `owedBack` = 927 − 227 = **$700.00**. `allocateRefund` computes `available` = 927 − 454 = $473.00, `collected` = $473.00, and returns `exceeds_collected` → 422.
7. But `recordReadjudication` already ran at `readjudicate/route.ts:109`, before the allocation. The statement now derives `patientResponsibility: $0.00`, `amountRefunded: $227.00`, `remaining: $0.00`. The patient is owed $700.00 that no code path will ever send, and every screen says the statement is settled.

Related, same function, opposite direction: a refund that goes `pending` → `failed` leaves the pending row counted forever (`status !== "failed"` excludes only the failed row). Capacity is permanently reduced by money that never left the provider. D-025's "a failed refund does not [count], because it claimed nothing" is stated as the intent and is not what the code does once two rows exist.

**Why the tests miss it:** `refund.test.ts:33-44` constructs one row per refund with a `hyperswitchRefundId` derived from the payment id. The pending case at line 113 uses a single pending row. The pending-plus-succeeded disagreement — the only shape production actually produces — is never built. This is precisely the blind spot D-026 names in its closing paragraph.

---

### 2. The intent route derives its balance without the payer correction — REAL BUG, over-collects

**`src/app/api/payments/intent/route.ts:117-122`**

```ts
const balance = deriveBalance(
  statement,
  existing,
  await refundsForPayments(existing.map((p) =&gt; p.id)),
);
```

The fourth argument is omitted. Every other caller passes it: `lookup.ts:116`, `statements/status/route.ts:43`, `receipt/page.tsx:82`, `readjudicate/route.ts:87`. The intent route is the **only** reader that computes the balance from the original line items, and it is the one that decides what to charge.

Sequence:
1. `AFT-4108-2290`, patient responsibility $927.00. Patient pays $500.00, succeeded.
2. Payer reprocesses; provider posts `revisedPatientResponsibility: 50000`. `overpaymentFrom(500_00, 500_00, 0)` = 0, so no refund is due and none is issued. Correct.
3. Statement page, status endpoint and receipt all derive `patientOwed` = $500.00, `remaining` = $0.00, status `paid`.
4. The patient still has `/statement/AFT-4108-2290/pay` open, or navigates back to it. `POST /api/payments/intent` with `{"portion":"full"}` derives `patientOwed` = **$927.00**, `remaining` = $427.00, and creates a real Hyperswitch payment for $427.00.
5. `resolvePayableAmount` never fires `statement_already_paid`, because in this route's view the balance is not zero.

The patient is charged $427.00 they do not owe, and the only recovery is the unauthenticated provider console. The `health_account` portion is wrong the same way: `healthAccountEligibleAmount(statement)` at line 127 is computed from the untouched line items and clamped against a `remaining` that is itself stale.

D-025 states the correction is applied when the balance is derived. It is, in four places out of five.

---

### 3. Re-adjudication has no idempotency; two views of the refund log disagree — REAL BUG, double refund

**`src/app/api/provider/readjudicate/route.ts:91` and `refund.ts:123-130`**

`overpaymentFrom(revised, before.amountPaid, before.amountRefunded)` uses `before.amountRefunded`, which counts **succeeded refunds only** (`balance.ts:151`). `allocateRefund`'s capacity check counts **non-failed** refunds. Two independent computations over the same log with different filters, and the window between them is the entire pending life of a refund — seconds for a card, days for ACH.

Sequence:
1. `AFT-4108-2290`, $927.00 collected on one card. Provider posts revised $700.00. `owedBack` = $227.00. Refund created at the processor, appended as `pending`.
2. Before `refund_succeeded` arrives, the same request is submitted again — a double-click on the provider form, a retry after a timeout, or two operators.
3. `before.amountRefunded` = $0.00 (nothing succeeded yet) → `owedBack` = $227.00 again.
4. `allocateRefund`: `alreadyRefunded` = $227.00, `available` = 927 − 227 = $700.00. $227.00 ≤ $700.00, so it allocates.
5. `createRefund` is called a second time. The patient receives $454.00 back on a $227.00 overpayment.

The capacity check only saves you when `owedBack` happens to exceed the remaining headroom. It does not here, and it will not for any correction smaller than half the collected amount.

Note the interaction with finding 1: they are not the same bug, and fixing `alreadyRefunded` alone makes this one *worse*, because capacity would then be $700.00 in step 4 for the right reason.

---

### 4. Nothing enforces the in-flight guard the code claims — REAL BUG, double charge through 3DS

**`src/lib/domain/balance.ts:29-37` vs `src/lib/domain/intent.ts:53-60`**

```ts
/**
 * Statuses that mean money is in flight. A statement with a payment in one of
 * these states must not accept a second full-balance payment, or a patient who
 * refreshes during a 3DS challenge pays twice.
 */
const IN_FLIGHT: ReadonlySet&lt;Payment["status"]&gt; = new Set([...]);
```

`IN_FLIGHT` is used at `balance.ts:178` to derive the `payment_pending` **status string** and nowhere else. `resolvePayableAmount` refuses only `transferred` and `remaining === 0`. `payment_pending` is not checked. The invariant in the comment is not implemented anywhere.

D-027's reuse path does not cover it, and says so: `requires_customer_action` is deliberately excluded from `REUSABLE` (`intent/route.ts:43-46`) — which is exactly the 3DS state the comment names.

Sequence:
1. Patient confirms; Hyperswitch moves the payment to `requires_customer_action`; the issuer's 3DS page loads.
2. Webhook records `requires_customer_action`. Derived status is `payment_pending`, `remaining` still $927.00 (nothing succeeded).
3. Patient loses the 3DS window, reopens `/statement/AFT-4108-2290/pay` in a new tab.
4. `POST /api/payments/intent`: no `REUSABLE` row exists (the only row is `requires_customer_action`, excluded), `remaining` is $927.00 → a **second** full-balance intent is created.
5. Patient completes both. `deriveBalance` sums two distinct `hyperswitchPaymentId`s — correctly, per D-017 — giving `amountPaid` $1,854.00.
6. `remaining` = `clampToZero(927_00 − 1854_00)` = $0.00, status `paid`. The $927.00 over-collection is silently absorbed.

D-023 and D-027 document *duplicate intents* as a known cost, framed as orphaned `requires_payment_method` rows and reconciliation noise. Neither documents a double charge, and neither addresses the 3DS path. I am treating the double charge as a bug rather than a documented limitation.

**Secondary, same site:** `money.ts:35-37` says "Callers that need to know the difference check for it before clamping rather than inferring it from the clamped value." No caller does. `overpaymentFrom` exists and is only ever reached through the provider console. An over-collection has no signal anywhere in the patient view, the receipt, the status endpoint, or the risk screen.

---

### Lower severity

**`src/lib/domain/refund.ts:81-88`** — `allocateRefund` takes raw `payments` and filters `status === "succeeded"` without folding. Today one processor payment yields one succeeded row, so it is latent. If a payment ever produces two succeeded rows with distinct `updatedAt` (a dashboard webhook resend carrying a new `event_id` and an advanced resource timestamp passes the stale check at `webhooks/hyperswitch/route.ts:80` and appends), `collected` double counts and the route authorizes a refund larger than what was collected. Every reader must fold; this one does not.

**`src/lib/domain/risk.ts:104-109`** — `declineMix` iterates raw `payments` while `attempts`/`failures` in the same function fold by `hyperswitchPaymentId` (lines 74-82). The decline histogram can exceed the failure count it sits next to. No money impact, but it is the same inconsistency inside a single function.

**`src/lib/webhooks/parse.ts:110`** — `resourceTimestamp` falls back to the envelope `timestamp` when the resource carries no `updated`/`updated_at`. The intent row stores the resource clock (`intent/route.ts:228`). A webhook that takes the envelope fallback is compared against a resource timestamp — two clocks, the class of bug D-018 fixed. Envelope time runs later than resource time, so it errs toward "accept", which is safe for a genuine event and unsafe for a stale redelivery.

**`src/app/api/webhooks/hyperswitch/route.ts:113`** — `currency: "USD"` is hardcoded on the ledger row while `parse.ts:177` extracts the real currency and discards it. An amount is recorded without validating the unit it is denominated in.

**`src/app/api/webhooks/hyperswitch/route.ts:112`** — records `event.amount` (the intent amount) rather than an amount-received field. Safe under `capture_method: "automatic"`; it would silently over-record under partial capture.

**`src/lib/domain/balance.ts:80-89`** — once `amountRefunded &gt; 0`, `deriveStatus` can never return `paid` again. A statement that is refunded and then re-paid in full reads `partially_refunded` permanently. Display only.

---

### Category verdicts

- **1. Unfolded log reads:** two found. `alreadyRefunded` (finding 1, fires on every refund) and `allocateRefund`'s `settled` (latent). Plus `risk.ts` `declineMix`, non-money.
- **2. Arithmetic:** clean on rounding, floats and signs. No float ever re-enters a calculation; `formatUsd` is display-only and correctly marked. `Cents` is enforced at every boundary and `readjudicate` rejects non-integers. The failures are in the *inputs* to correct arithmetic (findings 2 and 3), not the arithmetic. One clamping issue: `clampToZero` at `balance.ts:163` hides over-collection with no caller checking first, contradicting its own contract.
- **3. Client-influenced amounts:** **clean.** D-015 is implemented properly. The body carries only `portion`; `parsePortion` rejects anything else; both amounts are server-derived. The statement comes from the signed cookie, not the body. `readjudicate` takes a server-side operator amount, correctly out of the patient path (and its lack of auth is a stated boundary in D-025, not a bug).
- **4. Races:** three. Concurrent intents (finding 4, and the general read-then-write with no lock, which D-027 explicitly declines to solve). Refund allocated twice (finding 3). **Concurrent webhooks are clean** — two deliveries for one payment can both pass the stale check and both append, but the fold makes that harmless, and `claimEvent` is correctly set-if-absent (`store.ts:141-144`).
- **5. Paid without a verified webhook / walking backwards:** **clean.** `deriveBalance` counts only `succeeded`; the intent row is written with `confirm: false` so the create response can never be `succeeded`; the fold plus the stale check prevent a late delivery from reversing state. The only false `paid` comes via finding 4's clamp, which is an over-collection rather than a phantom payment.
- **6. Swallowed errors:** the two `catch` blocks are both deliberate and correct. The intent reuse fallthrough (`intent/route.ts:177-181`) is D-027 and fails toward taking the payment. The partial-refund 502 (`readjudicate/route.ts:143-159`) reports what already went out. One narrow ledger/processor divergence: if `appendRefund` throws after `createRefund` succeeds, the refund exists only at the processor — but the `refund_succeeded` webhook self-heals it. Same shape at `intent/route.ts:242-249`: `appendPayment` then `indexPayment`; a failure between them throws before the client secret reaches the browser, so the patient cannot confirm. Low.
- **7. Trusting payload before signature:** **clean, and carefully done.** `request.text()` is read raw at line 180, `verifySignature` runs at 183 over those exact bytes, `JSON.parse` is not reached until 200. Verification is HMAC-SHA512 with a length check before `timingSafeEqual` (`verify.ts:41`), so a malformed header cannot throw. The 401 is the only non-2XX and changes no state. `claimEvent` is deliberately called *after* verification and parsing so a forgery cannot burn a real event id. `describeSignatureShape` logs shape, never value.

### Documented limitations I am explicitly *not* reporting as bugs

Orphaned intents from remounts and portion changes (D-023, D-027); no client-supplied idempotency key on the intent write path (D-027); `readjudicate` unauthenticated (D-025); `transferred` unreachable (`balance.ts:72-78`, SCOPE item 7); empty licensed BIN table (D-021); `CreateIntentRequest.amount` now unused in `types.ts:254-258` (D-015); no rate limiting on lookup (D-012); "refunded" status while the patient still owes the original amount (`balance.ts:81-84`).</result>

There it is — and it's exactly what I was about to warn you about.

**Production needs one environment variable right now.** The whole patient path is down:

```
Missing required environment variable(s): AFTERCARE_STAFF_PASSWORD
```

Add to Vercel, type Secret, all three environments, then redeploy:

```
AFTERCARE_STAFF_PASSWORD
```

Any password you'll remember — it gates `/provider`. That single variable brings back lookup, checkout, and the console.

The failure is loud and names the variable, which is D-014 working, but it's still down until you add it. My fault for pushing a required variable without flagging it in the same breath.

## Why I did it

**The provider console was exposed on the live URL.** Verified against production before the fix: `POST /api/provider/readjudicate` and `POST /api/provider/risk` both reached input validation with no credential. Anyone who guessed the path could issue refunds or **turn off the fraud guard on your merchant account**.

`SCOPE.md` item 10 deferred console auth, arguing a fake login isn't authentication. Sound reasoning, applied to the wrong thing: a deferral describes something not built, and this was something built, working, and open. Now behind a shared password compared in constant time, exchanged for an HMAC-signed session. Per-user identity and an audit trail stay deferred — honestly this time.

Confirmed closed in production: `/provider` → 307, both writes → 401.

## Four money bugs, none caught by 128 passing tests

Two agents independently found the first one, which is why I believed it before verifying:

1. **The intent route was the only one of five `deriveBalance` callers that ignored a payer correction** — and it's the one that decides what to charge. A corrected-down statement showed the right number everywhere and would have charged the original residual again. Exactly the failure D-023 claims was closed.
2. **`alreadyRefunded` summed refund rows without folding** — third instance of D-017/D-026, in the last money computation not yet audited for it.
3. **Re-adjudication decided from *settled* refunds while allocating against *claimed* ones** — submitting the same correction twice inside the webhook window sent the money twice.
4. **An `IN_FLIGHT` set whose comment promised a 3DS refresh couldn't double-charge, and which enforced nothing.**

All four live in seams between two pieces of code that each look correct. Fixtures don't produce seams, because whoever writes one builds both sides to agree. 140 tests now.

## Still open from the audit

Two things I haven't touched, both yours to decide:

**The orchestration argument is the weakest point.** `DOMAIN.md` §7 builds a five-point case for why an orchestration layer beats a direct integration — processor plurality, vault portability, least-cost routing, failover, centralised retry. None is demonstrated. `DESIGN.md` §11 explicitly promises a second connector and a routing rule; that appears in no SCOPE deferral. It's a promise with neither delivery nor deferral, and it's Juspay's core value proposition. Either build a routing rule or add it to SCOPE with the same quality of reasoning as the other ten.

**`statement_descriptor` is claimed and never sent.** `DESIGN.md` §10 lists it under "what we send"; `createPayment` doesn't send it. The receipt tells the patient "Charges appear as NORTHGATE HEALTH" — currently false. The one field the docs argue hardest about is the one not wired.

Want me to take those two next?

---

## The three agents earned their keep

**One live security hole.** Both provider write endpoints — refunds, and the fraud-guard toggle on your merchant account — were reachable by anyone who guessed the path. `SCOPE.md` deferred console auth arguing a fake login isn't authentication. Sound reasoning, wrong target: **a deferral describes something not built; that was something built, working, and open.** Now behind a staff password with an HMAC session. Confirmed: `/provider` → 307, writes → 401.

**Four money bugs that 128 passing tests never saw.** The one two agents found independently: the intent route was the only one of five `deriveBalance` callers ignoring a payer correction — and it's the one that decides what to charge. Plus a third instance of the fold bug, a re-adjudication that decided from settled refunds while allocating against claimed ones, and an `IN_FLIGHT` set whose comment promised a 3DS refresh couldn't double-charge while enforcing nothing.

All four live in seams between two pieces of code that each look correct. Fixtures don't produce seams, because whoever writes one builds both sides to agree. That's now written down as a rule, not just an observation.

## Both gaps closed, and the routing one is a good story

**The descriptor is sent.** Verified on a live production payment: `statement_descriptor_name: 'NORTHGATE HEALTH'`. The receipt's promise to the patient was false until an hour ago — the one field the docs argue hardest about, in a HIPAA argument and as chargeback prevention, was the one field not populated.

**The routing rule is real and active.** No documentation for the request shape existed, so I asked the API — the same technique as D-007 and D-028. Each malformed request named the next missing field, including an error that enumerated every algorithm type. The full shape is in D-031 because it isn't written down anywhere I could find.

`routing_ejjXIFOEagrouU0O7K0s`, advanced, balances over $500 on their own branch, read live on the risk console. **It says plainly what one connector can't prove** — it can't demonstrate choosing between processors — and the second connector is now deferred in `SCOPE.md` item 11 with real reasoning, rather than sitting in `DESIGN.md` as an unkept promise.

A side effect: the API accepted `{"connector": "stripe"}`, independently confirming it's not the dummy connector. Second time that's been answered by something working rather than by looking.

Two things I got wrong along the way and corrected: I pushed a required env var without warning you, which took production down until you added it. And I twice declared a deploy "live" by checking a route that already existed.

## What's left before you submit

1. **Your 3-page architecture doc** — the largest item, explicitly yours
2. **Read the transcript**, then one line un-ignores it
3. **One payment on a real handset** — the only cold-test item I couldn't verify, and it settles the wallet question too

`AFT-4021-8837` is paid-then-refunded, the other two are clean and payable.

And every `serverEnv()` call sits inside a function body, so a missing variable surfaces as a request error rather than a cold-start crash.

1. **Fix verification** — are the five fixes real and complete, and did they break anything

I'll summarise each in my own words when they land, and verify anything load-bearing before acting — the first round claimed four money bugs and all four turned out real, but the round before that had an agent report a "confirmed API fact" that contradicted another documented fact, which is why D-007 exists.

One thing worth flagging now, while we wait: the third agent is scoped to attack `DOMAIN.md` and `DESIGN.md` as *arguments*, and to judge whether `DECISIONS.md` reads as genuine judgment or as performance. That's the one most likely to come back with something you'd disagree with — 31 decision entries is a lot for a one-week prototype, and "over-documented" is a defensible criticism. If it says cut, I'll bring you the case rather than start deleting.

<result>## Verdict on the five fixes

### 1. `alreadyRefunded` fold — **INCOMPLETE**

The reported symptom is fixed, the underlying class is not.

Correct parts: the fold is applied (`C:\Users\yun\dev\aftercare-payments\src\lib\domain\refund.ts:66`), pending still counts and only `failed` is dropped (`refund.ts:67`), and `allocateRefund`'s payment-side filter now folds by `hyperswitchPaymentId` before filtering to `succeeded` (`refund.ts:124-126`).

The gap is on the **refund-to-payment binding**, `refund.ts:63`:

```ts
const mine = refunds.filter((r) =&gt; r.paymentId === payment.id);
```

`payment` here is the *folded* row — the single survivor of `latestPerProcessorId`. `deriveBalance` (`balance.ts:140`) and `settledActivity` (`balance.ts:205`) both deliberately build `myIds` from **every** row for precisely this reason, and their comments say so outright: "a genuine refund_succeeded webhook was ignored exactly this way." `allocateRefund` is now the only reader of the refund log that still narrows the payment side to one row. This is the D-026 / commit `22f4d10` class, un-audited in the function D-029 claims to have audited.

Failure sequence (double refund of the same dollars):
1. Payment `pay_1` on statement S. Intent row written by `intent/route.ts:261-273` with id `a`, status `requires_payment_method`, `updatedAt` epoch.
2. Provider applies a correction. `allocateRefund` folds to row `a` (only row), issues refund `ref_1` for $200, `readjudicate/route.ts:198-207` writes a pending refund with `paymentId: "a"`.
3. `payment_succeeded` webhook lands. `webhooks/hyperswitch/route.ts:116-138` appends a **new** row id `b` for the same `pay_1`.
4. `refund_succeeded` webhook lands. `applyRefund` binds via `forPayment.find(p =&gt; p.status === "succeeded")` (`route.ts:161`) → row `b`. A second refund row now carries `paymentId: "b"`.
5. A second correction arrives. `allocateRefund` folds `pay_1` → row `b`. `alreadyRefunded(b, refunds)` sees only the row bound to `b`. The pending row from step 2 (bound to `a`) is invisible; only the succeeded one is counted, so this particular ordering under-counts by whatever was bound to `a`.

The cleanest reachable variant is webhook re-ordering: if `refund_succeeded` arrives before `payment_succeeded` (the ledger then holds only the intent row, and `applyRefund`'s `?? newest` fallback at `route.ts:162-165` binds the refund to row `a`), the later fold to row `b` leaves `alreadyRefunded` returning **0** for a payment that has already had its full amount returned. Capacity resets to the whole payment and a second correction can refund the same money again.

No test covers a refund bound to a non-folded payment row — `refund.test.ts:39-48` binds `tworows` to the same id the folded payment carries.

### 2. `deriveBalance` readjudication argument — **VERIFIED**

`intent/route.ts:126-131` now passes `await readjudicationFor(statement.id)`. Full call-site sweep, all five pass it:

- `src/app/api/payments/intent/route.ts:126`
- `src/app/api/provider/readjudicate/route.ts:100`
- `src/app/api/statements/status/route.ts:39`
- `src/app/statement/[ref]/receipt/page.tsx:82`
- `src/lib/domain/lookup.ts:116` (`viewStatement`, which is what `statement/[ref]/page.tsx:42` and `pay/page.tsx:42` both read through, so neither page is a separate omission)

`settledActivity` and `latestAttempt` take no readjudication parameter and correctly should not — they return log rows, not derived amounts. Their three call sites (`receipt/page.tsx:83`, `pay/page.tsx:63`, `status/route.ts:46`) all fold the same way. Clean, nothing missed.

### 3. `claimedRefundTotal` in re-adjudication — **VERIFIED**

`readjudicate/route.ts:118` uses `claimedRefundTotal(payments, refunds)` in place of `before.amountRefunded`. The function itself is correct and, notably, does **not** have the defect from item 1: `refund.ts:84` builds `settledIds` from every payment row, matching `deriveBalance`'s convention. It folds by `hyperswitchRefundId` (`refund.ts:87-90`) and excludes only `failed` (`refund.ts:91`). `before.amountPaid` on the other side counts folded succeeded payments only, so the two sides of `overpaymentFrom` are consistent. Double submission inside the webhook window now computes `owedBack === 0` and returns the no-refund-due branch at `route.ts:138-146`.

### 4. `inFlightPayment` — **INCOMPLETE**

The status choice is defensible: `requires_customer_action` and `processing` (`intent.ts:94-97`) are disjoint from `REUSABLE` (`intent/route.ts:44-47`), so the guard at `route.ts:140-152` and the reuse path at `route.ts:187-212` do not fight each other. Three real problems remain.

**(a) It does not cover the window it was written for.** The most likely double-charge window is between the browser confirming and the first webhook landing. During that window the ledger holds only the intent row written at `route.ts:261-273`, whose status is `requires_payment_method` — not in `IN_FLIGHT`. Sequence: patient confirms a non-3DS card → charged at the processor → re-POSTs `/api/payments/intent` (double-click, remount, back button) → `inFlightPayment` returns null → `deriveBalance` still shows the full remaining (no succeeded row yet) → the reuse block calls `getPayment` (`route.ts:193`), sees `live.status === "succeeded"`, fails the `REUSABLE.has(live.status)` test at `route.ts:197`, falls through, and **creates a second full-balance intent**. The route has the authoritative live status in hand at line 197 and discards it instead of refusing.

**(b) It can block a statement indefinitely.** There is no expiry or liveness bound on the guard. `requires_customer_action` is only ever written by a webhook, and Hyperswitch's intent status does not transition on expiry — an abandoned 3DS produces no further webhook. The reuse path checks `live.expires_on` (`route.ts:194-195`) precisely because an intent can go stale without anyone being told; the in-flight guard does no such check. A patient who closes the issuer window is then permanently 409'd off their own bill with "Wait for it to finish before starting another." Nothing in the codebase clears it.

**(c) Split tender regresses, badly for ACH.** The guard is statement-wide rather than over-collection-aware. With ACH configured (per commit `c14c1ad`), leg 1 sits in `processing` for days; leg 2 — the whole point of the split-tender flow in `DOMAIN.md` section 5 — is refused for that entire period. A guard comparing in-flight amount plus requested against `remaining` would block the genuine double-charge without breaking this.

**(d) Zero test coverage.** `intent.test.ts` contains no `inFlightPayment` test at all (only `resolvePayableAmount` and `parsePortion`), which is striking given D-029's own closing argument about seams.

### 5. Staff gating — **NOT FIXED** (gates complete; the token check is bypassable)

Gate inventory is complete. Every provider entry point is covered, and there is no `middleware.ts`, so these are the only gates and none is missing:

- `src/app/provider/page.tsx:30` (redirect)
- `src/app/provider/risk/page.tsx:30` (redirect)
- `src/app/api/provider/readjudicate/route.ts:58` (POST, 401)
- `src/app/api/provider/risk/route.ts:52` (GET, 401) and `:113` (POST, 401) — both verbs
- `src/app/api/provider/session/route.ts` is the issuer

Extension is genuinely prevented: the expiry lives inside the signed payload (`staff.ts:47`) and is read only after the HMAC verifies (`staff.ts:57-65`), covered by `staff.test.ts:45-49`. CSRF is covered by `sameSite: "lax"` on the cookie (`session/route.ts:29`).

**But the token can be obtained without the password.** `isStaff` never checks that the payload is a staff payload — it accepts any string signed with `sessionSecret` whose payload ends in a future millisecond timestamp (`staff.ts:51-66`). `access.ts:32-35` mints exactly that shape, with the same key:

```
staff.ts:32   createHmac("sha256", serverEnv().sessionSecret)...   payload = `staff.${expiry}`
access.ts:29  createHmac("sha256", serverEnv().sessionSecret)...   payload = `${statementId}.${expiry}`
```

Verified by executing both functions against one secret: `isStaff(grantAccess("stmt_a1"))` returns **`true`**.

Failure sequence: any patient looks up any statement with a reference printed on paper plus a date of birth → receives `aftercare_access` → copies that cookie value into a cookie named `aftercare_staff` → holds full provider console access for the 30 minutes of the access TTL, including `POST /api/provider/readjudicate` (issues refunds) and `POST /api/provider/risk` (toggles the merchant fraud guard, edits the blocklist). Renewable indefinitely by looking the statement up again. This is the exact exposure D-030 says was closed, reachable through the patient front door.

The fix is a domain separator in the HMAC (sign `staff|${payload}` vs `access|${payload}`), or a `payload.startsWith("staff.")` check in `isStaff`, or a distinct key.

---

## New defects, ranked

**1. Critical — staff/access token confusion.** `staff.ts:51-66` and `access.ts:28-35`. Detailed above under item 5. Introduced by the D-030 fix reusing `sessionSecret` and a token grammar the access module already occupies. No test covers cross-token acceptance.

**2. High — `allocateRefund` still narrows refunds to one payment row.** `refund.ts:63`. Detailed under item 1. Introduced as an incomplete fix: the refund side was folded, the payment side was not brought in line with `balance.ts:140` / `balance.ts:205`.

**3. High — the in-flight guard misses the confirm-to-webhook window and discards the live status it already fetched.** `intent/route.ts:140-152` vs `:193-197`. Detailed under item 4(a).

**4. Medium — the in-flight guard has no liveness bound and no over-collection test.** `intent.ts:94-112`. Permanently blocks a statement after an abandoned 3DS, and refuses the second leg of a split tender for the whole duration of an ACH `processing` window. Detailed under item 4(b), 4(c).

**5. Medium — `AFTERCARE_STAFF_PASSWORD` was added to the shared `serverEnv()` bundle.** `env.ts:75`. A variable needed only by the provider console now gates the entire patient flow and webhook ingestion: `access.ts:29` (statement lookup), `intent/route.ts:163`, `hyperswitch/client.ts:81`, and `webhooks/hyperswitch/route.ts:188` all call `serverEnv()`, and any one missing variable throws for all of them. Failure sequence: the variable is not set in the deployment environment → every statement lookup 500s → and `webhooks/hyperswitch/route.ts:191` returns 500 to Hyperswitch, which retries on an escalating schedule for 24 hours while no payment state is recorded. `env.ts:100-113` makes exactly this argument for keeping `storeEnv` separate ("a missing Redis configuration should break the ledger rather than the whole application"); the same reasoning applies here and was not followed. Note `.env.example` ships the value as `xxxxxxxxxxxxxxxx`, which `require_` at `env.ts:29` deliberately rejects, so a half-filled copy trips this.

**6. Low — the password comparison leaks its length, and a test comment asserts it does not.** `staff.ts:35-40` short-circuits on `left.length !== right.length` before `timingSafeEqual`. That is a length oracle, bounded but real. `staff.test.ts:33-37` claims the opposite: "cannot leak length through timing either." Hashing both sides before comparison removes it.

**7. Low — no rate limiting on `POST /api/provider/session`.** A single shared secret with unlimited online guesses. `SCOPE.md` item 9 already books rate limiting as missing for lookup; the new endpoint inherits the gap and is a higher-value target.

**8. Low — three comments now contradict the code they sit above.** `readjudicate/route.ts:38-41` ("NOT AUTHENTICATED, and that is a stated prototype boundary"), `risk/route.ts:28-29` ("Same stated boundary as the rest of the provider surface: not authenticated"), and `provider/page.tsx:21-23` ("Unauthenticated, and said out loud"). Each sits directly above a working gate. In a repo whose own D-030 finding is that a stale "Missing" row went unread, a stale "not authenticated" comment is an invitation for someone to delete the gate as dead weight. The risk console table (`risk/page.tsx:109-112`) and `SCOPE.md` item 10 were both updated correctly; these three were missed.

## Categories that are clean

**Circular import: no runtime cycle.** `balance.ts:16` is `import type { Readjudication } from "./refund"` — type-only and fully erased, and `tsconfig.json` sets `isolatedModules: true`, which forces exactly this syntax. The runtime edge runs one way only: `refund.ts:1` and `intent.ts:20` import the value `latestPerProcessorId` from `balance.ts`, and `balance.ts` imports no value from either. `store.ts:7` is likewise `import type`. Nothing breaks at runtime and nothing is hidden from the compiler.

**`serverEnv()` at module scope: none.** Every call site is inside a function body — `access.ts:29`, `staff.ts:32` and `:44`, `client.ts:81` and `:128`, `intent/route.ts:163`, `webhooks/hyperswitch/route.ts:188`, `pay/page.tsx:80` (inside the async component). The one module-scope export, `publishableKey` at `env.ts:134`, reads `process.env` directly with a `?? ""` fallback and cannot throw. No build or cold-start hazard. (The *coupling* concern is defect 5 above, which is a different problem from a module-scope throw.)

**Statement descriptor and routing code: clean.** `statement_descriptor_name` is sent at `client.ts:151` from a single constant, `STATEMENT_DESCRIPTOR = "NORTHGATE HEALTH"` (`fixtures.ts:29`), 16 characters against the documented 22-character cap. The receipt renders the same constant (`receipt/page.tsx:188`) rather than a duplicated literal, so the claim D-031 says was false cannot drift false again. No PHI enters the processor: `description` carries only the provider name and the opaque `statement.ref` (`intent/route.ts:221`), and `metadata` carries only `statement_ref` (`client.ts:155`). `activeRouting` (`client.ts:301-306`) tolerates both the bare-array and wrapped shapes, and its caller at `risk/route.ts:84-89` degrades to `null` with a warning rather than failing the screen. One cosmetic note, not a defect: `GET /routing/active` is not scoped to `hyperswitchProfileId`, so on a multi-profile merchant it may report rules that do not apply to this profile.</result>

All three agents are back and everything they found is fixed, verified, and deployed. 148 tests.

## The one that matters most

**A patient's access cookie was a valid staff session.** I verified it with a test before believing it:

```
isStaff(grantAccess("stmt_4021"))  →  true
```

`access.ts` and `staff.ts` signed with the same key, neither used a domain separator, and `isStaff` never checked the payload was a staff payload. The grammars were compatible, so the signature matched. Any patient who looked up any statement got a cookie that — renamed from `aftercare_access` to `aftercare_staff` — granted refunds and control of your merchant account's fraud guard. Reachable with a reference printed on paper.

**That is the exposure D-030 closed, reintroduced through the patient front door by the fix for it.** Now domain-separated with a payload prefix check, both regression tested.

The lesson is narrower than "separate your keys": two token families signed with one key are only safe if no payload from one can parse as a payload from the other — and nobody checks that when adding the second family.

## Three more of mine, from the same round of fixes

- **The event claim was taken before the ledger write.** One transient store failure burned the `event_id`, answered Hyperswitch's retry as a duplicate, and dropped a succeeded payment permanently. Released on failure now.
- **The staff password went into the shared `serverEnv` bundle** — which is what took production down earlier. `env.ts` argues that exact case for Redis one function above. Now `staffEnv`.
- **My in-flight guard was worse than the bug it fixed.** It broke split tender for the entire ACH `processing` window and could lock a patient out of their bill permanently after an abandoned 3DS. The question was wrong: not "is something in flight" but "would this plus what's in flight exceed what's owed."

**D-029 closed four bugs and opened two, one critical.** None of those fixes were verified against the running application, because they looked obviously correct. That's now written into the log as the finding it is.

## Also fixed

The risk console counted every abandoned intent as a successful payment. The receipt claimed "the difference was returned to the card you paid with" even when the refund was pending, failed, or never due. The return page redirected split-tender leg two to a receipt for leg one. Backticks rendering literally on screen. A US patient asked for their "billing postcode."

Plus the counts: README pointed reviewers at a console that's needed a password since D-030 without saying so, `DESIGN.md` claimed five routes and listed one never built (there are seven), D-025 had no reversal note, D-029's title said three bugs and described four.

## What I have not done, because they're your calls

The hostile reviewer graded it **pass, ~7.5/10 — strong on domain judgment, weaker on platform depth** — and raised three things I'd want your decision on:

1. **No reconciliation path.** The return page polls a ledger only a webhook can move, so it's a longer wait for the event it's meant to be a fallback for. `getPayment()` already exists and isn't wired to recovery. Called this the single highest-value change left.
2. **ACH is live with no return handling.** A patient pays $1,639 by ACH Monday, it returns NSF Thursday, and the statement says paid in full forever. `SCOPE.md` item 2 describes the fix and it isn't built. Its words back at us: that's exposed, not deferred.
3. **It thinks the repo is over-documented** — specifically `HANDOFF.md` and `DESIGN-SYSTEM.md` as padding for a payments role, and that `DECISIONS.md` is over-narrated.

I disagree with cutting the decision log, and I'd want to argue that with you rather than start deleting. Which of the three do you want to take?

It duplicates `DESIGN.md` and `DECISIONS.md`, it has gone stale four separate times in this session, and every time I fixed it that was maintenance on something no reviewer needs. `DESIGN-SYSTEM.md` is 175 lines of contrast ratios and type scales in a submission for a **payments** role. It's good work and it's off-brief.

The log is the evidence of judgment — it's where D-015, D-018 and D-026 live, and D-015 alone would carry an interview.

**What I think the real problem is:** not volume, framing. The README presents eight documents in a flat table as if equally worth reading. A reviewer with one hour doesn't need a smaller repo — they need to be told which two to read and in what order. That's a five-line fix to the README, not a deletion.

So my recommendation: **reorder and annotate rather than cut**, move `HANDOFF.md` out of the reader table into a footnote as "build brief, kept for provenance," and leave the decision log entirely alone. Cheap, and it removes the ratio problem without losing anything.

Both fixes are in and deployed. 162 tests.

## Reconciliation

The gap was circular and worth naming: `/pay/return` polled the derived statement status, which only a webhook can move — so the documented "webhook never arrives" fallback was waiting on the exact event it was meant to compensate for. Not a fallback, just a longer wait, ending in a promise of a receipt nothing could deliver.

`getPayment` had been in the client since step 2 and was never wired to recovery. Now `POST /api/statements/reconcile` asks the processor directly, and the return page calls it once *before* giving up.

It's a repair, not a second source of truth — reconciled rows carry the processor's own `updated` timestamp and go through the same fold, so a later webhook wins or loses on that field exactly as another webhook would. Both are asking the processor the same question, so they can't disagree.

## The ACH settling state

`StatementStatus` gains `settling`, checked *before* `paid`. A bank debit succeeds at submission and can be returned days later, so `paid` was stating something the rail hadn't decided.

This turned out to be your own D-030 rule applied to its author: `SCOPE.md` deferred the whole thing, but ACH was enabled and rendering in checkout. **A deferral describes something not built; this was built, reachable, and wrong.**

`deriveBalance` takes the clock as an argument rather than reading it — its purity is why the state machine is testable without a processor, and I wasn't willing to trade that for one parameter.

Two things I deliberately did not overclaim: nothing consumes a returned-debit event yet, so the statement leaves `settling` because the window expired rather than because the bank said anything. And five days is labelled as the simplification it is — NACHA is two banking days for most return codes and sixty for an unauthorised debit, so a real implementation holds per code, not per rail. `SCOPE.md` item 2 now says exactly that instead of deferring everything.

The patient-facing payoff is the bit worth demoing: a card receipt says paid, a bank debit receipt says it's clearing, can still be returned, and that they'll be told if it is.

## On the third item

I didn't cut anything, because I don't think volume is the problem — and I'd rather show you the README reframing than argue it abstractly. My proposal is: annotate the documentation table so a reviewer with an hour knows `DOMAIN.md` and `DECISIONS.md` are the two that matter, and move `HANDOFF.md` out of the reader list into a provenance footnote. Five lines, nothing deleted. Say the word and I'll do it — or leave it until the build is finished, since you want the log held until then anyway.

**What's left before submission:** your 3-page doc, reading the transcript, one payment on a real handset. The build itself is now steps 1–8 complete with every review finding closed.

## What changed

The README's documentation section is now a **path** rather than a flat table:

- **Ten minutes** — `DOMAIN.md` §2 (the one constraint everything follows from) and `DECISIONS.md` D-015 (the bug that best explains how this was built), then pay a statement on the live site
- **An hour** — four documents in reading order, each with a line on why it's there
- **Reference, not reading** — `types.ts`, `DEVELOPMENT.md`, `DESIGN-SYSTEM.md`
- **Provenance** — `HANDOFF.md` and `ai-sessions/`

`HANDOFF.md` moved out of the reader list. It's a build brief an agent executed, it duplicates `DESIGN.md` and `DECISIONS.md`, and it went stale four separate times in this project — listing it beside the domain analysis as if a reviewer should work through it was the actual mistake. It's kept, labelled as provenance.

`DESIGN-SYSTEM.md` stays and now says outright that it's off-brief for a payments role. That's more honest than quietly hoping nobody notices, and the reasoning in it is the same reasoning as everywhere else.

`DEVELOPMENT.md` is now linked from somewhere, which it wasn't — the audit caught it as an orphan.

## One thing I found while in there

**The built table was under-claiming.** The risk console, the live Hyperswitch blocklist and routing, reconciliation, and the ACH settling state were all missing from the one table a reviewer reads first. `/provider/risk` is a 328-line console with its own tested domain module and a genuine API-documentation finding behind it (D-028), and the README didn't mention it existed.

## What I actually need from you

**Does the Hyperswitch card form render at all?** That's the one thing I could not verify. My test browser got a 429 from `beta.hyperswitch.io` after hammering it all day — the assets serve fine to a clean client, so it should work for you, but "should" is why this step exists. If the panel is blank or zero-height, that's a real finding.

**Do Apple Pay or Google Pay appear above the card fields?** Use Safari on iPhone or Chrome on Android — the wallet only shows in its own vendor's browser. This settles `DESIGN.md` §15 open question 3, which has been open since build step 2. Either answer is useful:

- **They appear** — the Unified Checkout argument in `DESIGN.md` §3 pays off, and I'll record it as demonstrated
- **They don't** — almost certainly Apple Pay needing domain verification, which is a documented deferral rather than a defect. Tell me which browser and I'll write it up correctly

**Response**

Both fixed and live. You can see it in the screenshot — "Cosmetic consultation, not covered" wraps across three lines and the **Not HSA/FSA** badge now sits underneath it rather than chasing the last word.

They were the same mistake in two places: **an annotation sharing a line with the thing it annotates.**

**The badge** was `inline-block` with `white-space: nowrap`, so it competed with a wrapping service description for the same line and landed wherever the break happened to fall. On a wide screen that looked deliberate; on a phone it read as part of the service name. It's on its own line now.

**The ledger note** was separated from its label by a single space, so "Plan discount" and "your plan's contracted rate" ran together into one phrase — exactly what you saw. It's now a caption on its own line, and I shortened it to **"the rate your plan negotiated"**, which says the same thing without the possessive stacking.

Verified in the production render:

```
Plan discount
the rate your plan negotiated          -$1,028.00
```

The table also confirms it's scrolling inside its own box rather than pushing the page — columns cut at the right edge, zero horizontal overflow on the document.

Good to hear the phone run worked otherwise. Two things I'd still like from it when you get a chance, since they're the last open questions in `DESIGN.md` §15:

- **Did the Hyperswitch card form actually render** — I still can't verify that myself
- **Did Apple Pay or Google Pay show up** above the card fields

If you got as far as paying, `AFT-4108-2290` should now show $225.00 outstanding with a receipt naming the health account card.

### 46. Prompt

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. **Primary Request and Intent:**

The user is preparing a Juspay interview take-home submission: `aftercare-payments`, a US healthcare patient-billing prototype (post-adjudication patient responsibility flow) built on Juspay Hyperswitch, deployed at https://aftercare-payments.vercel.app.

Explicit requests across the conversation, in order:
- Read the repo state and plan next steps; document the AI session for the interview deliverable
- Build step 4 (real payment against a real statement)
- Consider building in the YUNVO design methodology/brand, because "rn it just looks like any other ai model built it"; also review system design/architecture
- Build step 5 (webhooks), then split tender UI, then decline handling, then refunds
- Fix remaining known gaps (intent idempotency, README, ai-sessions index, mobile)
- Build step 7 risk controls as "a whole screen for risk"; "move whatever relevant into this one singular repo… basically extract the relevants"
- Step 8 cold test; "do we have things properly documented and reviewed with subagents to catch the things that are off?"; "i do want to have this prepped to submit"
- "lets do one more overall sub agent review on everything being good first"
- Keep the decision log ("decision log should go in still i believe but after we finish building this whole project"); fix the three flagged items; explain why I thought it over-documented
- "readme reframing first"
- "how do i do mobile" → then reported mobile UI issues
- **Current:** "give me a thing to test" plus a bug report: on `AFT-4021-8837` (already settled, $0.00) the "Pay this balance" button still renders and is clickable — "it doesnt take me anywhere after but this is i think bad design"

2. **Key Technical Concepts:**
- Next.js 16 App Router (async `params`, async `cookies()`, route handlers vs RSC layers instantiating modules separately)
- Hyperswitch: Unified Checkout SDK, payment intents, webhooks (HMAC-SHA512 over raw body, `x-webhook-signature-512`), refunds, blocklist API, Smart Router routing algorithms
- Append-only ledger in Upstash Redis; statement status **derived** by folding, never stored
- `latestPerProcessorId` fold — every reader must fold identically (source of D-017, D-026, D-029 bugs)
- Branded `Cents` type; integer minor units only
- Domain-separated HMAC tokens (`aftercare.access.v1` / `aftercare.staff.v1`)
- Two-ground design system: instrument (dark, grotesque) = what you operate; document (cream, serif) = what you read
- Measured contrast ratios, semantic-only colour
- BIN classification for HSA/FSA (not a payment method)
- Empirical API resolution (probing malformed requests to discover shapes) — D-007, D-028, D-031

3. **Files and Code Sections:**

- **`src/lib/domain/types.ts`** — the interface contract, user-owned. Recently changed (approved) to add `settling`:
```ts
export type StatementStatus =
  | "open"
  | "payment_pending"
  | "settling"     // bank debit collected but not final
  | "paid"
  | "partially_refunded"
  | "refunded"
  | "transferred";
```

- **`src/app/statement/[ref]/page.tsx`** — **the file with the current bug**. `DueHeader` has branches for `settling` and `paid`, then a default branch. `partially_refunded` (AFT-4021-8837's status) falls to the default and renders "Pay this balance" despite `remaining === 0`:
```tsx
function DueHeader({ balance, statementRef }: { balance: StatementBalance; statementRef: string }) {
  if (balance.status === "settling") { ... }
  if (balance.status === "paid") { ... }
  return (
    <>
      <p className="eyebrow">{balance.amountPaid > 0 ? "Remaining balance" : "Amount due"}</p>
      <p className="answer">{formatUsd(balance.remaining)}</p>
      ...
      <Link href={`/statement/${encodeURIComponent(statementRef)}/pay`} className="btn">
        Pay this balance
      </Link>
```
Also contains `LedgerRow` (just fixed so `note` renders as a block caption) and `deduction()`.

- **`src/lib/domain/balance.ts`** — `deriveBalance(statement, payments, refunds, readjudication = null, asOf = new Date().toISOString())`, `latestPerProcessorId`, `settledActivity`, `latestAttempt`, `ACH_RETURN_WINDOW_DAYS = 5`, `withinReturnWindow`. `deriveStatus` checks `settling` before `paid`.

- **`src/lib/domain/refund.ts`** — `allocateRefund` (health account drawn last), `claimedRefundTotal`, `alreadyRefunded` (now matches every log row of a processor payment), `overpaymentFrom`.

- **`src/lib/domain/intent.ts`** — `parsePortion`, `resolvePayableAmount`, `inFlightPayment`, `wouldOverCollect(payments, remaining, requested)` (amount-aware guard replacing the blanket one that broke ACH split tender).

- **`src/lib/staff.ts`** / **`src/lib/access.ts`** — domain-separated HMAC signing. `staff.ts` also has `constantTimeEquals` using SHA-256 digests to avoid a length oracle.

- **`src/lib/domain/reconcile.ts`** (new) — `needsReconciliation`, `reconciledRow(known, live, observedAt)`; repair path for undelivered webhooks.

- **`src/app/globals.css`** — design tokens with measured ratios; `.flag-warn` recently changed to `display: table; margin-top: 0.4rem` so the badge sits under the description.

- **`README.md`** — documentation section reframed into ten-minute path / hour path / reference / provenance.

- **`docs/DECISIONS.md`** — 34 entries (D-001 … D-034).

4. **Errors and fixes:**
- **Heredoc backslash collapse** in the Bash tool broke several writes → worked around with `@NL@` placeholders and switched to the Write tool for large files.
- **D-013:** Map in module scope invisible across Next layers → diagnosed with per-instance IDs, moved ledger to Redis, made access tokens stateless.
- **D-014:** `?? "http://localhost:3000"` fallback sent a successful production payment to a dead redirect → removed fallback, read validated `serverEnv().appUrl`.
- **D-015:** `{"amount": 927.00}` created a 927-cent payment → removed the number; client names a portion.
- **D-017 / D-026 / D-029 / D-032:** four separate instances of reading the append-only log without folding identically.
- **D-018:** intent row stamped with our clock made every genuine webhook read as stale → store the processor's `updated`.
- **Pushed `AFTERCARE_STAFF_PASSWORD` as a required var without warning → took production down.** User had to add it. I acknowledged this as my fault.
- **D-032 (critical):** `isStaff(grantAccess("stmt_4021"))` returned `true` — a patient's access cookie renamed to `aftercare_staff` was a valid staff session granting refunds and fraud-guard control. Verified by test before fixing; fixed with domain separators plus a payload prefix check.
- **My in-flight guard (D-029) was worse than the bug it fixed** — blocked ACH split-tender leg 2 for days and could permanently lock a statement. Replaced with `wouldOverCollect`.
- **User feedback on mobile:** "not hsa/fsa is just slightly weird? like placement odd relative to the service" and "text of: plan discount your plans contracted rate is also odd" → both were annotations sharing a line with what they annotate; badge moved to its own line, ledger note made a block caption reworded to "the rate your plan negotiated". Verified live.
- **Twice I declared a deploy "live" by checking a route that already existed** — a flawed verification method I acknowledged.

5. **Problem Solving:**
Steps 1–8 of the build order are complete. Six subagent reviews run in two rounds; every finding verified before acting (per HANDOFF's "verify before trusting" rule) and every one fixed. 162 tests passing, build clean, deployed. Real sandbox artifacts: payment `pay_hz3mLO8GL41QrB5AgEN1` ($32.70), refund `ref_gmey0mFKKTTx5cRWbhBa` ($12.70), routing algorithm `routing_ejjXIFOEagrouU0O7K0s`, blocklist guard enabled with BIN `411111` blocked.

**Response**

Fixed, verified live, pushed as `4c41b34`.

**What was actually wrong.** The pay route asked *"is anything owed?"*. The header asked *"is the status the string `paid`?"*. Those are different questions, and `partially_refunded` answered them differently — so the header offered a button the route refused. Both were individually defensible; the divergence was the bug. It's now one function, `isCollectible()` in [balance.ts:289](src/lib/domain/balance.ts:289), called by both, so they can't disagree again. Logged as D-035 — the fifth instance of this same shape after D-017, D-026, D-029, D-032.

The header now reads as a settled statement rather than a broken one: "Settled, after a correction", what you paid, what came back, and a receipt link.

---

## One thing to fix before you submit

**There is no way to reset the fixtures.** No script, no provider control, nothing in `store.ts`. The ledger is append-only in production Redis, so the first reviewer who pays `AFT-4108-2290` consumes it for everyone after them. `AFT-4021-8837` is already spent — that's why it's settled.

You have **two payable statements left, permanently**. If you test both yourself, your interviewer opens the live site to three settled statements and never sees a payment flow.

I'd add a staff-only reset before submitting. Say the word and I'll build it.

---

## Test script

Do these **in order**, and stop before the ones marked ⚠️.

**1. The fix (free, repeatable)**
`AFT-4021-8837` / `1988-03-14` → header says "Settled, after a correction", no pay button. Manually hit `/statement/AFT-4021-8837/pay` → bounces back. This is the one I just fixed.

**2. Decline handling (free — declines don't consume a statement)**
`AFT-3994-1177` / `1962-05-27` → pay → card `4000 0000 0000 0002`. Expect a normalized, actionable message, not a raw processor code. Then retry on the same statement to confirm it's not locked.

**3. Blocklist (free)**
Any statement → card `4111 1111 1111 1111`. BIN `411111` is blocked in the live Hyperswitch blocklist, so this should be refused before it reaches the connector. Cross-check at `/provider/risk`.

**4. Risk console (free)**
`/provider/risk` → confirm the blocklist and routing algorithm are reading live from Hyperswitch, not hardcoded.

**5. Mobile (free)**
`AFT-4021-8837` at 375px. You already checked the ledger note and the HSA badge; the settled header is new, so it's worth one look.

**⚠️ 6. Split tender — consumes `AFT-4108-2290`**
`1975-11-02` → $927.00, mixed HSA eligibility. Pay the eligible portion with `5555 5555 5555 4444` (classifies as a health account card), then the remainder with `4242 4242 4242 4242`. This is your strongest flow and the one most worth leaving intact for the interviewer.

**⚠️ 7. Refund — needs a settled statement**
`/provider` → simulated payer correction issues a real partial refund. Only works against something paid.

---

My recommendation: run **1–5 freely**, they cost nothing. Leave **6** alone until you've decided about the reset — it's the flow that best demonstrates the work, and right now it's a single-use demo.

Confirmed — this is a genuine bug and it's client-side. `checkout.tsx:89-124` fetches the intent once and never again. When `confirmPayment` fails without redirecting (a blocklist rejection fails immediately), the component keeps the *same* `clientSecret`. Pressing Pay again re-confirms a payment Hyperswitch has already marked `failed`, which it refuses.

Our API already handles this correctly — `failed` isn't in `REUSABLE`, so it would mint a fresh intent. The client just never asks it again.

**Response**

Both handled, pushed as `393d8a5`.

## The blocklist error was a real bug

You'd have hit this on **any** decline, not just the blocklist. The blocklist just makes it easy to reach, because a blocked BIN is refused instantly with no redirect, leaving you sitting on the checkout.

`checkout.tsx` fetched the intent once. Hyperswitch closes a payment when confirmation is refused, so pressing Pay again asked it to confirm a payment it had already marked `failed`. The intent route was already correct — `failed` isn't in `REUSABLE`, so a fresh request mints a new payment. The client simply never made one.

Now a failed confirmation re-requests the intent, and **the server decides** whether the old one is still usable: still confirmable → same secret back, so a mistyped card keeps what you typed; closed → a fresh payment. No status-guessing on the client, which would have been another instance of the two-readers problem I just fixed in D-035.

**I did not take `manual_retry`, which is what the error message suggests.** That setting reuses one payment id across attempts refused for different reasons, so a blocklist rejection and a real issuer decline collapse into one row. The ledger folds on payment id, so that buys a retry at the cost of the ledger's ability to say what happened. A new intent per attempt is the truer record. That reasoning is in D-036 — it's the kind of thing worth having an answer to if they ask why you didn't just flip the profile setting.

The decline text also moved above the remount boundary; it used to be state inside `PayButton`, which the new mount destroys, so the explanation vanished exactly when you got a fresh form.

## Wallets

Agreed, and I've recorded it as a finding rather than a shrug. It was an open question in `DESIGN.md` §15 — now closed by observation, since your checkout renders Card and ACH Debit only. I confirmed the same thing live:

The reason is worth stating because it's not a code decision: the SDK renders what the connector has enabled. Apple Pay needs a domain association file on the merchant's own domain plus registration with Apple, Google Pay needs a merchant id from their console, and both then need enabling on the connector. None of that is integration work, and none of it belongs on a `vercel.app` subdomain. That's `SCOPE.md` item 12, and it makes the same point your Unified Checkout argument makes in `DESIGN.md` §4: a wallet is configuration, not an integration.

## What I could not verify

**I did not confirm the retry works end-to-end.** The card fields live in a cross-origin Hyperswitch iframe that my browser automation can't type into or even see in the accessibility tree. What I did confirm is that the new client code is in the deployed bundle (`2_fy9j-eaqgj5.js` contains the new copy) — that proves the deploy landed, not that the behaviour is right.

**Please re-run your blocklist test.** Same statement, same `4111 1111 1111 1111`, then press Pay now a second time. You should get "That payment was not accepted", the processor's reason, and a working form — not the `manual_retry` message. If it still fails, tell me what it says.

Also worth knowing: I saw a `429` from Hyperswitch in the console during testing, from repeated intent creation. Harmless now, but if the card form ever fails to render for you, that's the likely cause — wait a minute and reload.

Your two payable statements are both still intact; declines don't consume them. The fixture reset I flagged earlier is still unbuilt and still worth doing before you submit.

**Response**

The retry works — that's the D-036 fix confirmed. But the message you pasted is itself a defect, so I fixed it. Pushed as `3b607ca`.

**Three sentences, two of them telling you to retry, none saying why.** The middle one — "Payment failed. Try again!" — is the SDK's. Mine wrapped it.

So I asked the API what it actually knew about the three real failed payments on your account:

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| `error_message` | "We're unable to accept this card, please try another card or a different payment method" |
| `error_message` | "Payment declined: Card declined" |
| `error_code` | null on all three |
| `unified_code` / `unified_message` | null on all three |

Two things came out of that. The processor's text was **more specific than the SDK's and we were discarding it**. And `unified_code`/`unified_message` are confirmed empty on real failures — which upgrades the note in `decline.ts` from repeating the docs to something checked against your account.

**The classifier got the important case wrong.** `"We're unable to accept this card"` matched none of the existing needles and fell through to `unknown` — whose guidance is *"you can try the same method again."* That's a blocklist rejection. It will be refused every single time. The one decline your risk work exists to produce was the one we told the patient to retry.

Now it classifies as a new `card_not_accepted` category. I didn't reuse `card_blocked`, because that says *"contact your bank"* about a card the bank never saw.

**It deliberately doesn't say why.** Telling someone testing stolen cards which control stopped them tells them what to vary next. A test asserts the copy never says blocklist, blocked, fraud, risk, "your bank", or "issuer" — while still offering a bank account as an alternative, which is the useful half.

What you'll see now:

> **That card cannot be used for this payment**
> Another card or a bank account will work. Nothing has been charged. If no card works, the billing office can take the payment another way.

Classification moved server-side into the intent route, which already retrieves the payment, and only runs on a retry so a first mount doesn't pay for it. That also closes the "two sources of decline language" gap I'd flagged as unresolved in D-036 — the webhook path and the confirm path now both go through `classifyDecline`.

170 tests, clean tree.

**Two things worth flagging:**

I added a `card_not_accepted` member to the `DeclineCategory` union in `decline.ts` and four optional error fields to `HyperswitchPayment` in `client.ts`. Neither is `domain/types.ts`, so I didn't treat it as touching your contract — but the union has an exhaustive switch, so you own whether that category should exist.

And `AFT-3994-1177` now has three failed attempts against it. Harmless for the ledger, but if you demo that statement, its history shows the declines.

Same test as before to confirm: blocked card, then Pay again. Still unbuilt and still worth doing before submission: the fixture reset.

**Response**

Built, deployed, and gated. The 404 → 401 transition confirms the deploy actually landed.

**It's in the billing office console** at [`/provider`](https://aftercare-payments.vercel.app/provider), below the re-adjudication form. Two-step: "Reset the fixtures" → a warning saying exactly what will happen → confirm. I can't run it myself since it needs the staff password, and I'm not going to ask you to put that in chat.

**The decision you picked has a nice property.** The reset doesn't remember `AFT-4021-8837`'s paid state — it retrieves `pay_hz3mLO8GL41QrB5AgEN1` and `ref_gmey0mFKKTTx5cRWbhBa` from Hyperswitch and builds the rows from the responses. The two ids are the only remembered state; amount, status, tender, and both timestamps come from the processor. So if the sandbox account is ever replaced, the ids stop resolving and it reports a partial reset instead of inventing a statement that was never paid. `rebuildIsSound` refuses unless the processor still says: payment succeeded, refund succeeded, refund belongs to that payment, refund is partial.

I confirmed `GET /refunds/{id}` against your live account before writing it rather than assuming it mirrors the payments path. It returns `created_at`/`updated_at`, which the rebuilt row carries — otherwise a later webhook would be ordered against our clock and reintroduce D-018.

**Two things I found while building it that you didn't ask about:**

`clearFixtureLedger()` takes no arguments, deliberately. Every key comes from the `STATEMENTS` constant or a list reached from one. A staff-authenticated endpoint that deletes whatever it's handed would be a worse thing to have built than the problem it solves.

And there were **28 Redis keys against 12 ledger rows** — payment indexes outliving the lists that created them. An index is how a webhook finds its statement, so a stale one is a route by which forgotten payments reappear in a ledger you just emptied. The reset sweeps those, but only where the value names a fixture statement.

Webhook idempotency claims and lookup-failure counters are kept, per your call.

**I documented it as the least defensible thing in the repo**, in D-038 and SCOPE item 13. Deleting a payment ledger is the opposite of what a ledger is for, and every derived balance here is trustworthy *because* the log only grows. The honest fix is per-visitor ledger isolation; I didn't build that because it touches every read path to solve a problem that only exists because this is a demo. Better to name the tradeoff than hide it — and if an interviewer asks "why is there a delete on your append-only ledger," the answer is already written down.

179 tests, clean tree, pushed as `8cf4b0a`.

Run it when you're ready — it'll clear the three failed attempts sitting in `AFT-3994-1177`'s history and hand back both payable statements. Tell me when you have, and I'll read the ledger back and confirm all three landed in the right state.

The reset didn't run — that's a payment test you pasted, not a reset result. Here's how I know: the ledger **grew** from 28 keys to 30, and `stmt_4021` still has its original two rows (`requires_payment_method` + `succeeded`). A rebuilt statement would have exactly one payment row, because the rebuild never creates an intent row. `stmt_3994` still has all eight old rows plus two new ones from `pay_Rs2ORO0JuSWLDt1pfpHr`.

So the reset button is still unpressed. It's at [`/provider`](https://aftercare-payments.vercel.app/provider), below the re-adjudication form — you need to be signed in with the staff password, then "Reset the fixtures" → confirm.

**What you did test worked correctly.** The decline path ran end to end:

```
pay_Rs2ORO0JuSWLDt1pfpHr  failed  163900  tender=standard_card  reason=insufficient_funds
```

The webhook arrived, classified the decline, stored the category, and the return page rendered the normalized guidance. That's the intended behaviour.

One thing worth noticing in the older rows — the two blocklist rejections stored `reason=unknown`:

```
pay_2KZ9xh5TLfNLsCRL2rxy  failed  reason=unknown
pay_nkpNbVnpkhsn5r9DKx0B  failed  reason=unknown
```

That's the bug D-037 fixed, preserved in the ledger. Those were classified before the fix, so they're stamped with the old wrong category. A new blocklist decline will store `card_not_accepted`. Since the ledger stores the category rather than the sentence, those two rows will keep rendering the old "you can try the same method again" guidance forever — the reset clears them, which is one more reason to run it.

Also: 20 payment-index keys against 13 actual payments, so seven orphans have accumulated. The sweep will take those.

**One small thing I noticed in what you pasted.** The hero says "You are back. Now we wait for the ledger." while the panel directly below says definitively that the payment was declined. The heading is written for the waiting state and doesn't update once the answer arrives, so the page reads as though it's still waiting for something it already knows. Want me to make the hero resolve with the answer?

| Statement | State | Verified |
|---|---|---|
| `AFT-4021-8837` | Settled after a correction, $0.00 remaining | Statement page + receipt both render |
| `AFT-4108-2290` | `open`, $927.00 | Lookup returns clean |

**30 keys → 8.** The ten junk rows on `stmt_3994` are gone, including the two stamped with the pre-D-037 `unknown` category. Nineteen orphaned payment indexes swept, one kept — the rebuilt payment's, which is what a future webhook needs to find its statement.

**The run exposed one defect, now fixed and pushed as `7541d68`.** The receipt read:

```
refund issued                              September 5, 2026 12:36 AM UTC
"insurer reprocessed this claim on"        September 6, 2026
```

Money returned before the reason for it existed. Every other field came from the processor, but I'd stamped the correction with the reset's own clock. It now dates from the refund's `created_at` — which is what that timestamp approximates anyway, since `recordReadjudication` runs immediately before `createRefund` in the live console. Nothing in a rebuilt statement is dated by us now, which is what the design was reaching for and hadn't quite reached.

That fix only affects the *next* reset. The current data still carries the September 6 correction date, so run the reset once more when convenient and it'll be internally consistent. Not urgent — the amounts and the fold are all correct either way; it's the narrative date that's off by a day.

Both payable statements are intact and the demo is clean for submission. Still outstanding from earlier: your 3-page architecture doc, and reading the transcript before removing `.gitignore:35` to ship it.

# Aftercare: Architecture & Technical Design

## 1. The Industry and Why

Standard retail payment assumptions break down completely in healthcare because the price is unknown at the point of service. **Response**

Audit done. Here's everything, in priority order.

## Secrets: clean, but I found one real leak and fixed it

The literal-value scan (grepping the whole repo for every value in your `.env.local`) found **`KV_REST_API_URL` in the transcript** — one of my probe scripts errored earlier today and printed the Upstash host in a stack trace. Not a token, but it's the datastore address and shouldn't ship.

I added all ten infrastructure secrets to `.redactions` and re-exported. Re-scanned clean. The only remaining hits are `.redactions` itself, which is *supposed* to contain them and is gitignored at `.gitignore:38`. Both it and the transcript are confirmed untracked.

Everything else flagged was a false positive: `.env.example` placeholders, `whsec_test_secret_value` in a test, npm integrity hashes, and Hyperswitch's own `dev_xxxxxxxx` doc samples.

Also confirmed absent: your private global CLAUDE.md preferences (0 hits), any system-prompt content (0 hits), profanity (0). Only two emails appear — yours, from git config, and Anthropic's from commit trailers. Both already public on your commits.

## The transcript was stale

It was exported Sept 4 and contained **none** of D-034 through D-038. Re-exported: 54 prompts, 20,128 lines, 152 redactions.

I updated `ai-sessions/README.md`, which was still describing "33 prompts, steps 3 through 6" and stopping at five moments all from the first half — the review rounds were invisible. Added four: the auth bypass a test confirmed before anything changed, the guard whose fix was worse than the bug, the five-times two-readers pattern, and the decline text. **Every prompt reference in that file is now verified against the transcript**, including the pre-existing ones.

## On your ARCHITECTURE.md — two things worth fixing

**BIN is Bank Identification Number, not "Bin Identification Number"** (§3). Small, but it's a payments company reading it.

**§4 claims "Card & Bank Debit Processing | Built"**, while your own `docs/SCOPE.md:23` says ACH is *"Connector configured, untested."* The doc claims more than the repo does, and a reviewer comparing the two will find the contradiction. Your `settling` state and the receipt language are real; what was never exercised is an actual ACH payment.

Lower priority: the **risk console is absent** from §4 — live blocklist and routing read from Hyperswitch was step 7 and it's substantial work that's currently invisible. Your deferred table lists 2 items where SCOPE has 13, and your own README calls the deferrals "the honest half." And there's one em dash on line 37, against the repo's stated no-em-dash rule.

Everything else I checked was accurate: `aftercare_access`, the 5-day window, `x-webhook-signature-512`, all four route paths, the portion enum, newest-`updatedAt` folding, SAQ A, and the BNPL reasoning.

## Your call on the gitignore

I haven't touched `.gitignore:35`. Your own index sets the bar: *"kept out of version control until it has been read end to end."* That's 20,128 lines, and I'd rather say plainly that automated sweeps caught credential *shapes* and known strings, not judgment calls about what reads well. The scans are as thorough as I can make them; the read is still yours.

**Response**

Done — `a97b805`. Your prose and structure are untouched; these are corrections, not edits.

**Four factual fixes:**

**BIN** now expands to *Bank* Identification Number.

**The ACH claim.** Your table said "Card & Bank Debit Processing | Built" while `docs/SCOPE.md:23` says "Connector configured, untested." I split it into two rows so the card path keeps Built and ACH carries what the repo actually supports — the `settling` state and receipt language are real, but no ACH payment has run end to end and no returned-debit event is consumed. That contradiction was the most likely thing to get caught.

**Two built things were missing** from a built-vs-deferred table: the risk console and the reconciliation path.

**One em dash**, per your own no-em-dash rule.

**Three deferrals added**, taking you from 2 to 5. The important one is the **second connector**: processor plurality, failover, and least-cost routing are the strongest case for using Hyperswitch at all, and one connector demonstrates none of them. You have a routing algorithm configured and readable, but with a single processor it's a shape rather than a decision. Better that you say it than that they notice. The other two are rate limiting (you already count failed lookups; the throttle is what's missing) and disputes.

Cut any of the three if they overrun your page budget — they're self-contained rows.

**Still open, and both are yours:**

`.gitignore:35` — the transcript is clean by every scan I can run, but your own index sets the bar at "read end to end," and that's 20,128 lines.

The `AFTERCARE_STAFF_PASSWORD` goes with the submission, not in the repo — the README already says so, and the scan confirms it appears nowhere.
